*Prior art:* Go's `encoding/csv`; Elixir's `NimbleCSV` (a library, RFC 4180, a parser per separator); Gleam has no std CSV (`gsv` is a package); ReScript programs bind a JavaScript library (`papaparse`). Python's `csv` module writes `""` for a lone empty field for the same reason as `Format`.

## json

`Optional[T] = Absent | Present[T]` selects an absent or present record key.
`Nullable[T] = Null | NonNull[T]` selects null or the bare value. A Nullable
key is required. `Optional[Nullable[T]]` keeps all three states. `Option`
is unchanged. These are ordinary closed unions (exhaustive `match`), with
a codec representation selected in lowering, not a checker exception or
field annotation. The field's type after instantiation selects the form: a
field typed `T` in `Box[T]` omits its key when `T` is `json.Optional[int]`,
also inside a generic function, where the codec witness of `T` carries the
field form. `Optional` outside a record field keeps its tagged form;
there is no absent root or array element. Failure rows remain conservative
structural rows, including the union's possible shape failures.

`Writer.Null` writes a null event. `Reader.TakeNull` consumes null and
returns true, or leaves the next value and its path unchanged on false.
Both the JSON reader and codec's event replay support this event.

Effect Schema's intent is to distinguish absent keys from explicit null.
Ergo does not take schema classes, field annotations or runtime tag checks:
the field type and exhaustive unions express those states. ReScript uses
optional record fields and `Nullable.t`; Gleam separates `optional_field`
from `optional`; Go's `omitempty` and default Elixir maps have only one
empty-value policy, so a separate wrapper is needed for a third state.

`Value = Null | Boolean | Num | Str | Array | Object` is an untyped JSON
value (G4): a field the program passes on without reading its shape. It is
a wire type, alone or as a record field, and a program reads it with an
exhaustive `match`. `Object` is `[]Member` (`Member { Key, Value }`), so it
keeps the order of the members. `Num` keeps the spelling of a number;
`ParseNum` (which fails `Malformed` on a text that is not one JSON number),
`IntNumber` and `Num.Wire()` are the only ways in and out, so a `Num` is
always a valid number. The codec writes each kind as the bare JSON value,
not as a tagged record. The law `ValueRoundTrips`: `Encode(Decode(text))`
is `text` without insignificant white space, with strings in the encoder's
escaping. **Rule for a repeated key:** `Value` keeps every member, a
repeated key included, in order. It does not interpret keys, so it passes
on what it read; the row `JsonValueRepeatedKey` holds this. `Writer.Number`
writes a spelling as it is; `Reader.Peek` gives the next value's kind
(`WireKind`, a prelude union, the end of a list or object included) and
`Reader.Number` reads a spelling. Codec's `Replay` reads a tag as the member
`"_tag"` (`Key` leaves it, `String` reads it). Prior art: Go's
`json.RawMessage` keeps the bytes, unread; ReScript's `JSON.t` and Gleam's
`Dynamic` are trees that the host's parser builds, and they drop number
spellings; Elixir Jason keeps member order only with `objects: :ordered_objects`
and takes the last of a repeated key.

Std `json` (S-json) writes and reads the derived codec's events ([types.md, Codecs](types.md#codecs)) as JSON text (RFC 8259). It is the format Effect's `Schema.parseJson` gives a schema, without a schema value: the codec is the type's own.

| Name | Signature | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Codec` | `Codec[T wire] { MaxBytes int, MaxDepth int }` | Ergo |
| `Encode` | `Encode[T wire](c Codec[T], v T) string` | Ergo over `JSONTake`, `JSONGive` |
| `Decode` | `Decode[T wire](c Codec[T], text string) T fails Malformed \| wired[T]` | Ergo over `Open`, `decode[T]` |
| `Malformed` | `Malformed { Offset int, Reason string }` | `JSONMalformed` |
| `Text`, `NewText`, `NewHTMLText` | a sealed `Writer` with `Null()` and `Written() string` | `Text`, `NewJSONText`, `NewJSONHTMLText` |
| `Open`, `Reader` | `Open(text string, maxBytes int, maxDepth int) Reader fails Malformed`; `Reader` is a `Reader` | Ergo over `JSONOpen` and `Cursor` |

1. **Form** (owner q81). A record is an object whose keys are its field names with the leading capitals lowered (`Port` as `port`, `ID` as `id`, `HTTPStatus` as `httpStatus`; types.md, Codecs 2); a union value an object whose `"_tag"` member names its variant (Effect's form), so `Option` is `{"_tag":"Some","value":…}`; a `Map` an object in key order; a slice or tuple an array. An integer is its exact digits (the Effect RPC profile's ±(2^53−1) rule is the R units'). A float is written as `encoding/json` writes it (JavaScript's `Number` form), and NaN and ±Inf, which JSON has no numbers for, as the strings `"NaN"`, `"Infinity"`, `"-Infinity"`, which `Float` reads back (protobuf's JSON mapping), so `Encode` cannot fail.
2. **Strings.** `"`, `\` and the control bytes are escaped (`\n`, `\r`, `\t`, else `\u00XX`), U+2028 and U+2029 too; no HTML escaping (`encoding/json/v2`'s default), unless the Text is `NewHTMLText`'s (`<`, `>`, `&` as `\u003c`…, `encoding/json`'s default). A byte of invalid UTF-8 is the lone surrogate `\udcXX` of the byte (Python's surrogateescape, PEP 383), which `Decode` reads back as the byte, so every Ergo string, which holds bytes, round-trips; a valid UTF-8 string is plain JSON. Read strings are copied out of the text.
3. **Two phases.** `Open` checks the whole text: one value with white space around it, at most `MaxBytes` bytes (no limit when not positive) and `MaxDepth` levels of lists and objects (10000, `encoding/json/v2`'s, when not positive), else `Malformed` with the offset and the rule. Then `decode[T]` builds the value and fails only on its shape, with the member's path (`units[0].replicas`). Unknown keys are skipped and of two members with one key the last wins (`encoding/json`).
4. **Fast path.** An `encode(v, t)` whose `t` is a `Text` appends `v`'s bytes directly: per-type functions with the keys as quoted constants and the commas placed at compile time (`lower/wirejson.go`); a codec witness carries the fast function, so `Encode`, generic over `T`, takes it. The Writer path stays the reference: `-X …/lower.wireLowering=writer` lowers every encode through the `Writer` interface, and an encode whose writer is typed `Writer` takes it in every build.
5. **Laws.** `RoundTrips`, `FastAgreesWithWriter` (the fast path writes what the Writer path writes, in a `Text` and an HTML `Text`, inside events too), `TruncatedFails` and `DepthLimits`; lower `TestStdJSONLawsRun` runs them, with `JsonFastRefinedPair` for a refined field and a by-name pair, which std cannot declare.

*Prior art:* Go's `encoding/json` (reflection, a cached encoder per type, `checkValid` before decoding) and `encoding/json/v2` (`jsontext` tokens, depth 10000, no HTML escaping, duplicate names refused); serde_json's format-specialised `Serializer` the derive calls; Elixir's `Jason.Encoder` derive with pre-escaped keys; Gleam's `gleam/json` (encoders and decoders written by hand as values); ReScript's `JSON.stringify` and `rescript-schema`.

## Pure bodies

A std extern whose `runtime/v1` Go body is marked pure is transparent in footprints (wave std-1, unit G5b; [rows.md, Footprints](rows.md#footprints)). A call of it adds no capability, no task start and no opacity, so a function that calls `strings.TrimSpace` has the footprint of one that calls nothing: empty, with no entry poll. Every other extern call stays opaque.

1. The mark is the directive line `//ergo:pure` in the doc comment of an exported top-level function of `runtime/v1`. `v1.BodyOf` reads it and reports `Body.Pure`; the checker's footprint (`check/footprint.go`, `runs`) reads only that fact.
2. The mark is a reviewed claim. `BodyOf` refuses a marked body, with an error that names the rule, when:
   - it is a method;
   - its file imports a package outside `v1.PureImports` (`strings`, `strconv`, `unicode`, `unicode/utf8`, `unicode/utf16`, `slices`, `sort`, `math`, `math/bits`, `path/filepath`, `encoding/hex`, `encoding/base64`, `crypto/sha256`, `hash/crc32`) and outside `v1.PureImportHostMembers` (`time`, std-align S2b; `encoding/csv`, std-csv U4, with no host member: [csv](#csv) 5);
   - its body names a member of a `PureImportHostMembers` package that reads host state, as a package member or a method of that name: for `time`, `Now`, `Since`, `Until`, `Sleep`, `After`, `AfterFunc`, `Tick`, `NewTicker`, `NewTimer`, `Local`, `LoadLocation` and `Parse` (which reads the local zone for an abbreviation). `time` is not in `PureImports`, which is also the user extern list (G9): a user extern binds a whole package, so it could bind `time.Now`;
   - its body names a member of an allowed package that reaches the host, as `v1.PureImportsExcept` lists them (`filepath.Abs`, `EvalSymlinks`, `Glob`, `Walk`, `WalkDir`), or its file dot-imports such a package;
   - it takes a hidden argument (the frame, the call site, the task, the Scheduler, the reading package);
   - it takes a function, whose code is unknown;
   - its body names a top-level function or variable of `runtime/v1` (so it reaches no other body and no package state), starts a goroutine or uses a channel. A local name that shadows a top-level one is refused too.
   For a refused body `BodyOf` returns no shape, so the checker treats its call as opaque. A body that is not marked is not pure.
3. Purity is only std's. A user `extern go strings.TrimSpace` stays opaque: its Go body is outside the reviewed set.
4. An Ergo std function over pure externs, such as `strings.Cut`, is pure through its own footprint, which its package records.
5. Today the marked bodies are the bodies of `strings` and `strconv` in `text.go`, those of `slices` and `cmp` in `collections.go` named in [Slices, maps and cmp](#slices-maps-and-cmp) 6, those of `filepath` in `path.go`, those of `math` and `bits` in `numbers.go`, those of `hex`, `base64`, `sha256` and `crc32` in `digest.go`, those of `csv` in `csv.go`, the prelude's `Append`, `Get`, `Delete` and `Put` in `data.go` (lw-pure-prelude; `Put`, L2), and the prelude's `Unreachable` in `data.go`, which raises a defect and reaches nothing (a defect is no capability; the lowering panics at each call site instead of calling it, [types.md, Unreachable](types.md#unreachable) 6). The prelude's `Append`, `Get`, `Delete` and `Put` are intrinsics: the lowering writes a Go wrapper for each in the program (`Get` builds the program's `Option` from the comma-ok read that `v1.Get` is), and a marked intrinsic's wrapper is a pure function with no environment. So `func evens(n int) []int` that loops and calls `out.Append(i)` lowers to Go's `func evens(n int) []int` with no task poll, and `match m.Get(k)` in a function that does nothing else keeps it pure (`TestPurePreludeEmits`, `TestPurePreludeAgrees`). Their writes are the writes Go's `append` and `delete` make, on a source that no other binding sees ([types.md, Bindings](types.md#bindings) 2), so a pure function gives equal results for equal arguments. The reference lowering, in which their calls are opaque again, is selected with `-ldflags=-X=ergo.local/prototype/internal/sketch/check.pureCollections=off`, or in a test with `check.SetPureCollectionsForTest(false)`. `TestPureBodiesKeepTheAllowlist` reads every embedded runtime file and checks each marked body against the rules independently of `BodyOf`; `TestFootprintsPureStd` checks the footprints.

*Prior art:* GHC trusts a foreign import's declared type: an import whose result is not in `IO` is pure on the author's word. Gleam's and ReScript's externals carry no effect fact, and Elixir's compiler sees a remote call as unknown code. Koka infers effects only for code it can see, and its externs state their effect in the declared type. Ergo keeps the claim on the Go body, where a reviewer reads it, and checks its mechanical part: what the file can reach.

## http

G1's buffered HTTP server uses Go `net/http`; a response body may also be
streamed (Rm, item 9). TLS and HTTP/2 configuration are outside this core.

1. `Listen(addr) Listener fails ListenFailed needs Ambient` binds TCP;
   port zero is valid, and `l.Addr()` reports the bound address. The
   transport has no HTTP policy. `l.Close()` releases an unused listener.
   Every server protocol shares it: `rpc.Serve` takes an `http.Listener`.
2. `Serve[R](l, limits Limits, handle escaping func(Request) Response needs R | Streaming)`
   has `fails InvalidLimits needs R | Scheduler`. Serve binds the request's `Streaming`
   provider around each invocation, so a handler that does not stream is a
   function of a narrower row and passes unchanged. `Limits{MaxBodyBytes,
   MaxHeaderBytes, IdleTimeout, ReadHeaderTimeout, ReadBodyTimeout}` is
   required. Every value must be positive (zero is not "unbounded"); a
   refusal leaves the listener open. The header budget has Go
   `http.Server.MaxHeaderBytes` semantics, including its request-line
   accounting. This one Limits record is also taken by NewClient and
   NewNet. They validate all five fields, but use only MaxBodyBytes and
   MaxHeaderBytes to bound each response; their time limit is the caller's
   scope, not these three server-phase timeouts. A non-positive field is
   InvalidLimits wherever Limits is taken.

   **Connection phases** (E4). Serve bounds each read phase of a
   connection by its timeout: the header phase (from the accept, or from
   the first byte of the next request, until the whole header is read) by
   `ReadHeaderTimeout`, the body read by `ReadBodyTimeout`, and the wait of
   a kept-alive connection for its next request by `IdleTimeout`. The
   handler's own time is not bounded here. Each bound is a child task of
   the serving task that sleeps on the program's `Scheduler`, as the
   deadline of `timeout.After` does: a test `Scheduler` (`time.Manual`)
   decides when a phase expires, and Serve arms no host timer of its own.
   A phase that ends first stops its timer. Each connection has at most
   one live timer task: Serve joins the old timer before starting the next
   phase's timer. The timers are sequential children, not one re-armable
   child. An expired idle or header phase closes the connection with no
   answer; an expired body phase
   reaches the handler as `BodyReadFailed`, and its answer is the
   connection's last. A serving stop stops and drains the timers with the
   requests. Go: `http.Server.IdleTimeout` and `ReadHeaderTimeout` (on the
   host clock); Go has no body-only timeout. `TestHTTPPhasesExpireOnTheScheduler`
   (runtime/v1) holds the three bounds on a test Scheduler and counts live
   timer tasks without measuring host time.
   *Prior art:* Go `http.Server` deadlines on the connection; Node's
   `server.keepAliveTimeout` (5 s by default) and `headersTimeout`; Bandit
   and Thousand Island's `read_timeout`, one bound for every read; mist
   (Gleam) closes idle connections on a timer of its actor. Ergo keeps the
   three apart, as Go and Node do, and makes each required.
3. `Request{Method, Path, Query, Header, Body}` is buffered. Header and
   query values are slices of `Field{Name, Value}`, retaining repeated
   values. `Header(fields, name)` ignores case; `Query` uses exact names.
   Both return `Option[string]`, keeping a missing and an empty value apart.
   `Path` is Go's decoded URL path; matching does not clean it.
   **Typed query** (S-query). `DecodeQuery[T wire](fields) T fails QueryRepeated | wired[T]`
   reads a record from the query through the derived codec (types.md,
   Codecs), as `json.Decode` reads one from a text, so a query record
   follows the codec's one rule: a field's key is its wire name, an
   unknown key is skipped (as JSON's), a field the query does not name is
   `WireMissingField` unless its type is `json.Optional` (a list that may
   be empty is `json.Optional[[]T]`: the codec has no default value), and
   a value that does not parse is `WireWrongKind` at its key. A scalar
   field (a string, `true`/`false`, a decimal number, or a refined type
   over one) takes exactly one value: a key given twice is
   `QueryRepeated{Key}`, never first- or last-wins. A slice field takes
   every value of its key in query order. A record or union field has no
   query form (`WireWrongKind`). `ParseQuery(raw)` is the fields Serve
   gives `Request.Query` (Go's `url.ParseQuery`; a pair with a malformed
   escape is dropped, as Go's `URL.Query` drops it); the law
   `QueryDecodesByTheRule` sends generated query strings, with repeats,
   an unknown key, upper and lower case escapes, `+` and dropped pairs,
   through both and compares a hand reference. Effect's
   `HttpServerRequest.schemaSearchParams` decodes the search params with a
   Schema whose field is a string or an array, and a repeated key given
   to a string field is a parse issue; Ergo keeps that purpose without a
   Schema value: the record's type is the schema and the failure is a
   typed row. Go's `url.Values.Get` takes the first value; Phoenix's `Plug.Conn.Query` takes the last unless the key ends
   in `[]`; Gleam's `wisp` gives the list of pairs and leaves the choice
   to the handler.
4. `Body` is `Buffered{Text} | BodyTooLarge{Limit} | BodyReadFailed{Reason}`.
   No partial body is passed as a success. The application chooses a status
   for each refusal. Header parse errors remain Go's HTTP protocol refusals.
5. `Response{Status, Header, Body}` is buffered. An empty body has no
   content type. Go suppresses the body of HEAD while preserving the
   buffered response's headers. Status codes and response header validity
   have Go `net/http` semantics.
6. Every handler invocation is a child task of Serve. A client disconnect
   after a buffered read stops its child. A failed read still reaches the
   handler, since a peer may have closed only its write half. A serving
   stop closes the listener and connections,
   stops all live children and joins their releases and transport workers.
   A handler defect ends Serve and keeps the defect cause; it is not an
   undeclared typed failure or an invented application status.
7. `Match[T](routes, method, path)` returns `Found[T]{Route, Params}`, `NotFound`
   or `MethodNotAllowed{Allow}`. Routes are `Route[T]{Value, Method, Path, Status}`:
   `Value` is what the route stands for (an endpoint value), so a program
   reads the endpoint of a Found route as `f.Route.Value`, with no lookup
   and no impossible fallback (H2). `Allow` is the methods of the routes
   that take the path, each once, in route order, with HEAD after GET
   when GET serves it, so a 405's `Allow` header is `strings.Join(m.Allow, ", ")`
   (RFC 9110 15.5.6); the law `AllowIsWhatMatches` holds that a method is
   in Allow exactly when Match finds a route for it. Literal
   segments match exactly; `:name` captures one non-empty segment. Order
   breaks ties. An explicit HEAD route precedes the GET fallback. There
   is no automatic trailing-slash redirect. `BuiltPathMatches` is the
   construction law for parameter segments.
   `Status` is required and has no default. `Success(route, jsonText)` uses
   that status and `application/json`; 204 and 205 have no body or content
   type. A handler uses this helper for success and its own total catch for
   failures. `SuccessUsesRoute` holds this rule. `FailureStatus{Tag, Status}`
   tables give each nominal contract failure exactly one status (400..599).
   `StatusOf(table, tag)` is None for missing, repeated or invalid entries.
   `Failure(table, taggedJson)` preserves the body with application/json and
   reads the status from that table; invalid JSON, a missing tag or an absent
   status is a defect, not a default 500. Use the ordinary codec for a closed
   union or row value so the failure body has its nominal `_tag` and fields.
   The TypeScript HTTP projection checks exported literal tables against the
   contract's actual failure rows. `FailureUsesTable` tests lookup and response
   preservation. `Serve` stays independent
   of the router: it sends the response the handler returns.
8. `Files(root fs.Root, prefix string, r Request) Response` serves the
   files of a read-only folder (G1f). The name is `r.Path` after `prefix`,
   its empty and "." elements dropped; Files' work is lexical, and the
   confinement is the Root's (fs 20 and 21): `fs.OpenRoot(dir)` on the host,
   `fstest.NewTree` in a test. A method other than GET and HEAD is 405 with
   `Allow: GET, HEAD`; HEAD gets GET's headers through Serve (5). One rule
   parts 403 from 404: 403 when the name is refused, by Files (a ".."
   element, which reaches no read) or by the root (`Denied`, `Escapes`);
   404 when nothing servable is there (`NotFound`, `Failed`: a directory, a
   loop, no element left, a path without the prefix). Files lists no
   directory. A file is 200 with `ContentType(name)`, the media type of its
   extension ignoring case (text types with `charset=utf-8`, an unknown one
   `application/octet-stream`); an empty file has no Content-Type. The law
   `FilesAsksBeneath` observes, through its private law-only recorder, every
   path Files passes the root: at most one, relative, with no "", "." or ".."
   element, and none for a request path with a ".." element or without the
   prefix. The probe of `TestStdHTTPLawsRun` restates it and checks Files
   against a reference of the rule over a Tree with links out, in and
   looping (`FilesAnswersByTheRule`).
9. **Streamed bodies** (Rm; orchestrator option 1, 2026-11-10).
   `Stream[R](status, header, write func(w BodyWriter) fails WriteFailed needs R) Response needs R | Streaming`
   answers with status and header at once, then the parts `write` sends
   with `w.Write(text)`; each Write is flushed before it returns. Streaming
   is a capability, not a second Response form: a body that runs
   after the handler returns would need the handler's row inside the
   Response type (`Response[R]`), which a pure handler such as Files could
   not name. The rules: status and header are fixed before `write` starts;
   the BodyWriter closes when `write` returns, and a later Write fails
   `WriteFailed{Reason}` (one rule for a writer kept past its stream, under
   Serve and under a Recorder); a client that goes away stops the request
   task, so `write` ends at its next Write with the stop
   (`TestHTTPStreamHangUpStopsTheWriter`); a WriteFailed of `write` ends the
   stream; a second Stream in one request is a defect. After a stream, Serve
   sends nothing more: the response is the stream. An absent Content-Type
   is not sniffed, as for an empty buffered body. Under Serve the result's
   Body is empty; `NewRecorder()` is the test provider, under which Stream's
   result is the buffered response the stream sent, so a streaming handler
   is tested as a function (laws `StreamIsItsWrites`,
   `WriteAfterStreamFails`). Serve's provider writes through `Exchange`,
   the host's response side of one request, which only the transport makes
   (`TestHTTPStreamFlushesEachPart`). Effect's
   `HttpServerResponse.stream` takes a Stream value and a content type;
   Go's `http.Flusher` leaves the order of header, writes and end to the
   handler. Plug's `send_chunked`/`chunk` and Mist's `chunked` keep the
   connection in the handler's hands; here the request task owns it, so its
   stop is the hang-up.

10. **Client** (H1, G9; orchestrator contract 2026-11-10).
    `Do(r Request) Response fails RequestFailed needs Net` sends one
    buffered request through the bound `Net`, the operation type
    `Net { Send(r Request) Response fails RequestFailed }`. The host's
    provider is `NewClient(origin, limits Limits) Client fails InvalidClient | InvalidLimits needs Ambient`:
    `origin` is `scheme://authority` (http or https, no user, path, query
    or fragment), and `limits` is the required response budget (header
    and body, both positive; `InvalidClient{Origin, Reason}` otherwise).
    No request is made until a Send. Send joins `r.Path` (the decoded
    path, which the client escapes) and `r.Query` (in its order) to the
    origin and sends `r.Header` with the `Buffered` body; the Request type
    is the server's. `RequestFailed{Cause}` has the closed cause union
    `Refused{Reason}` (nothing accepted the connection),
    `Transport{Reason}` (the exchange broke or was not HTTP),
    `TooLarge{Limit}` (the response's header or body over its budget;
    never a truncated success) and `Malformed{Reason}` (the request cannot
    be sent: a method or header name that is not a token, a header value
    with a line break, a path without "/", a body that is not `Buffered`).
    One request is one exchange: a redirect is a response, and the body
    comes back as sent (no transparent decompression). There is no timeout
    and no retry: a stop of the caller's scope cancels the exchange and
    ends Do with the stop after the exchange let go; a caller adds a
    deadline (`timeout.After`) or a schedule. `InMemory(handle)` is a
    `Net` with no socket that calls `handle` under a Recorder, so a test
    binds a client to its server in process. The property
    `TestHTTPClientMatchesServer` sends generated requests to Serve on a
    loopback listener: each arrives as sent, and each generated response
    (3xx included) comes back as sent or TooLarge;
    `TestHTTPClientRefusedAndStopped` holds Refused and the stop, waiting
    on events. Effect `HttpClient`'s purpose is a request whose failures
    are typed and whose transport a test replaces; Ergo takes that through
    the operation type and the closed cause union, and leaves out its
    service tag and layers (a `context` binds Net), its request and
    response middleware (`filterStatusOk`: a status is a value the caller
    matches), and its schema-bodied responses (the caller decodes the
    buffered text). Gleam `gleam/httpc` `send` is the same buffered request
    in, response out, with its own error union (`FailedToConnect`,
    `ResponseTimeout`); Elixir `Req` follows redirects and retries by
    default and Finch keeps a pool and a receive timeout: here those are
    the caller's explicit choices. Go `net/http`'s `Client` is the host
    transport; one Go transport per header budget is shared, so idle
    connections are reused.

11. **Runtime URLs and redirects (CU5).** `NewNet(policy OriginPolicy,
    limits Limits, redirects RedirectPolicy) Remote fails InvalidClient | InvalidLimits needs Ambient`
    delegates host authority once, without choosing one origin. `OriginPolicy`
    is `AnyOrigin` or `Origins{Values []string}`; an empty set denies every
    origin. `RedirectPolicy` is `NoRedirects` or `Follow{MaxHops int}`; a
    negative MaxHops is InvalidClient, zero refuses the first redirect.
    `Remote` has private fields and provides the replaceable operation type
    `Network { SendURL(r URLRequest) Response fails RequestFailed | OriginRefused | RedirectLimit }`.
    `Fetch(r URLRequest)` calls that operation through the caller's context.
    The old fixed-origin Net, Do and Client keep their contract.

    `URLRequest { URL string, Method string, Header []Field,
    Credentials []Credential, Body string }` takes an absolute http(s) URL.
    It does not hide a URL inside the server Request's decoded Path.
    `ForOrigin(origin string, field Field) Credential fails InvalidClient`
    marks one header as a credential of that origin. Its private fields
    prevent a forged owner; no Ambient is needed to label data. Ordinary
    Header fields are public and may cross origins: secret headers belong
    only in Credentials, including Authorization, cookies and custom keys.

    Before the initial request and every hop, the provider checks the
    resolved origin against its policy. Refusal is `OriginRefused{Origin}`
    and makes no request to that origin. Identity includes scheme, host and
    effective numeric port; DNS case and default ports are normalized, IPv6
    addresses are canonicalized, and interface-zone case is retained.
    Credential fields are selected anew for each hop by exact origin, so
    an A-B-A chain sends A's fields only to A, and B's only to B. User
    information in a URL is refused, not converted to implicit credentials.

    **The request target** (S-target). The URL is read as the WHATWG URL
    Standard reads a special (http, https) URL: tabs and newlines are
    removed, leading and trailing controls and spaces are trimmed, "\" is
    "/", the dot segments (`.`, `..`, and their `%2e` spellings) are
    removed, and the path and the query are percent-encoded with the
    standard's path and special-query sets (a byte of invalid UTF-8 reads
    as U+FFFD). An escape the URL holds stays as it is, valid or not
    (`%zz`, `%2F`), and so does every byte outside those sets (`|`, `[`,
    `'` in a path). The request line carries that path and query as they
    are; the fragment is not sent. Only the authority (up to the first
    "/", "\", "?" or "#") goes through Go's `url.Parse` as before, so the
    origin, its policy, the credentials bound to it and the Host header
    do not change. A Location is resolved as the standard resolves a
    relative URL against the request's URL. Two cases are refused as
    Malformed, never sent changed: a request URL (or a Location of another
    scheme) whose scheme two slashes do not follow (the standard reads
    `http:x` as `http://x`; a Location `http:x` under an http URL is
    relative, as the standard says), and a path that starts
    with "//" and holds text Go's client cannot write in origin form
    (Go sends an `Opaque` that starts with "//" in absolute form; Go keeps
    such a path only through `RawPath`, as valid escaped text). The fixed-
    origin `Do` keeps its decoded `Path`, which the client escapes. Through
    an `HTTP_PROXY` for a plain-http URL, Go's client writes the target in
    origin form; this is a known gap. A 3xx with a `Location` that
    `url.Parse` refuses is a response, as any redirect is: the exchange
    uses the transport's `RoundTrip`, not `Client.Do`, which parses
    Location even when it follows nothing. `TestHTTPFetchSendsTheWebTarget`
    reads the request line and the Host header at a raw loopback listener
    for 400 generated URLs and 200 generated redirects, and compares them
    with targets recorded from a reference parser
    (`runtime/v1/testdata/webtarget/gen.mjs`: Bun 1.4.4's `URL`, which
    gives the same text as Node 24's ada).

    Follow handles 301, 302, 303, 307 and 308 with a Location, resolving
    relative URLs normally. Each followed redirect consumes one hop; an
    exhausted limit is `RedirectLimit{URL, Limit}`. NoRedirects returns
    the redirect response. A 303 changes the method to GET except HEAD;
    301/302 change a non-GET/HEAD method to GET. That change drops its body;
    307/308 retain method and body. Required header/body budgets apply to
    every response. There is no new timeout, retry or decompression. The
    existing exchange owns cancellation and joins it before a stop returns,
    including at a redirect target.

    `TestHTTPNetworkMatchesModel` compares generated chains, cycles,
    header sets and policies with a graph model on two loopback origins;
    `TestHTTPNetworkStops` checks completed cancellation after a redirect.
    `TestHTTPNetworkRuns` compares the source API and typed failure bridge
    with a model; a failing seed prints its source, outputs and replay.
    The Network exemption is for these external peer obligations, as Net's
    is; finite properties are tested, not proved. `examples/download`
    captures the delegated provider outside main and accepts runtime URLs.

    Effect `HttpClient.followRedirects` supplies the intent: a bounded
    traversal without leaking credentials. An ordinary operation type,
    explicit policy unions, typed refusals and captured provider replace
    service tags, layers and middleware combinators. Go's Client is the
    transport and hand-Go control: a redirect loop with the same policy,
    limits, header ownership and cancellation. No parity or speed claim is
    made; the matching benchmark harness and quiet-window receipt remain
    pending. Gleam httpc 5.0.0 exposes follow_redirects (off by default),
    lowering through its Erlang client; Elixir Req exposes redirect and
    redirect_trusted through BEAM library code. ReScript uses its host HTTP
    client. Go's auth-copy rule permits same-host/subdomain redirects;
    Ergo's explicitly marked credentials use the stricter exact origin,
    including the port, not Go's host-only trust rule.

12. **A body that goes to a sink (CU6).** `Network` has a second
    operation, `SendURLInto(r URLRequest, accept Accept, put func(part string) fails SinkFailed)
    Response fails RequestFailed | OriginRefused | RedirectLimit | StatusRefused | SinkFailed`
    (`accept` and `StatusRefused`: item 13).
    It applies the policy, credentials and hops of `SendURL`, then gives
    the final response's body to `put` while it arrives, one part at a
    time (at most 32 KiB), in order, on the caller's task. The returned
    `Response` has the status and header and an empty Body. The body of a
    redirect that is followed is not read. `FetchInto(r, put)` calls the
    operation through the caller's context. `FetchFile(root fs.HostRoot,
    path string, r URLRequest)` writes the body to a new file beside `path`
    below `root` and renames it over `path` only after the whole body
    arrived: a failure, a body over its budget or a stop removes the new
    file and leaves `path` as it was. A failed write is `SinkFailed`; a
    path that leaves the root is `fs.Escapes`.

    The sink is borrowed and the body is consumed inside the call: `put`
    and `root` stay the caller's, and no part of the exchange outlives the
    call. On every exit (the end, a failure, a failure of `put`, a stop)
    the request is cancelled and its reader joined before the call
    returns, so the connection is let go. A reader goroutine hands the
    parts over an unbuffered channel, so at most two parts are in memory
    whatever the size of the body. `Limits.MaxBodyBytes` still bounds the
    bytes a sink is given: a declared Content-Length over it fails
    `TooLarge` before any part; a body that grows over it fails `TooLarge`
    before the part that crosses it. A sink that wants a stop before the
    end returns `SinkFailed` from `put`.

    A returned `Seq` of parts was not taken: it would hold the connection
    after the call, which is an owned resource return (#197). When #197
    lands, a scoped `Seq` form can return a resource that the caller's
    scope releases; this form stays.

    `TestHTTPFetchIntoMatchesModel` generates body sizes around the part
    border, the peer's write sizes, declared and undeclared lengths, a
    redirect with a body of its own, budgets one under, at and over the
    size, and the two sinks; the sink must hold the bytes sent, each put
    must leave a prefix, and an over-budget body must fail `TooLarge`,
    give no part when its length was declared, and leave the directory as
    it was. `TestHTTPFetchIntoStops` stops a caller mid-body and waits for
    the peer's hang-up and the call's end; no file is left.
    `TestHTTPNetworkRuns` runs the source API through all three forms.
    `examples/download` saves with `--out`.

    Effect `HttpClientResponse.stream` supplies the intent: a body larger
    than memory, read as it arrives. Its `Stream` needs the response's
    `Scope` to hold the connection; here the body is consumed inside the
    call, so no scope or stream value is needed, and the failure of a
    sink is in the row. Elixir Req's `into:` takes a function, a
    collectable (`File.stream!`, used only for a 200) or `:self` (an async
    body that outlives the call); `FetchInto` is the function form and
    `FetchFile` the collectable form, for an accepted status (item 13), with the new-file
    rename that a collectable does not give. Gleam httpc is buffered only.
    Go's control is `io.Copy` from `resp.Body` into the file and a rename,
    which is what the runtime does on the same transport. No speed claim
    is made.

13. **The status a sink takes (CU6b).** A body reaches a sink only for a
    status the caller accepts. `type Accept = AnySuccess | Statuses{Values
    []int}`; `Accepts(a, status)` is the one rule: `AnySuccess` is 200 to
    299 and is the default of every sink form (`FetchInto`, `FetchFile`,
    `DoInto`, `DoFile`); `Statuses` takes exactly its values. Any other
    final status fails `StatusRefused{Status, Prefix}`, where `Prefix` is
    the first bytes of the body, at most 1024, for a message; `put` gets
    nothing, and no file is made or replaced. The redirect rule comes
    first: a redirect that `Network` follows is not a final status.
    `SendURLInto(r, accept, put)` takes the rule, and the fixed-origin
    `Net` has the same streamed operation, `SendInto(r Request, accept,
    put) Response fails RequestFailed | StatusRefused | SinkFailed`.
    `Client` runs it on the same streamed exchange as `Remote` (the
    runtime's `httpStreamURL`; a redirect is a response there, so its
    status meets the rule); the in-memory `Handler` answers, then gives an
    accepted body in one part. For a set other than 2xx, a caller calls
    the operation, and for a file `IntoFile(root, path, fetch)`, which
    `FetchFile` and `DoFile` are built on. `IntoFile` makes its new file
    at the first `Put` (or at the end, for an empty body), so a refused
    status touches no file; a new file that cannot be made fails with its
    `fs` failure.

    `TestHTTPFetchIntoMatchesModel` adds generated final statuses, the
    2xx rule or a generated set, and the fixed-origin client to item 12's
    property: a refused status fails `StatusRefused` with the status and
    the body's first bytes, gives no part and leaves the directory as it
    was; the peer sees no new file when the request arrives; a file below
    a missing directory fails `fs.NotFound`. The law
    `InMemorySendIntoIsSend` holds `Handler`'s `SendInto` and `Accepts`.
    `TestHTTPNetworkRuns` draws a final status and the two fixed-origin
    sinks, so the std callers of the rule run in a built program.

    Effect's `HttpClient.filterStatusOk` supplies the intent: a status
    the caller did not expect is a typed failure, not a body. Effect
    adds it as a client transform, and its `ResponseError` holds the
    whole response; here the rule is a parameter of the operation, the
    default is stated once, and the failure holds a bounded prefix, so a
    refused body never grows memory. Elixir Req writes an `into:`
    collectable only for a 200 and raises otherwise
    ([Req docs](https://hexdocs.pm/req/Req.html), read 2026-11-10). Go's
    control is a `resp.StatusCode` check before `io.Copy`.

Prior art for Files (source inspected, not measured): Wisp's `serve_static`
(main branch) drops every ".." substring from the decoded path, joins it to
the directory and reads through `simplifile`, so the guard is lexical and a
link below the directory is followed out; it adds `charset=utf-8` to text
types, as `ContentType` does. Go's `http.FileServer` refuses a ".." element
and serves `http.Dir`, which also follows links; Go 1.24's `os.Root`
(`root.FS()`) is the resolution `fs.HostRoot` uses. Elixir's Plug.Static
refuses ".." segments in the request (by its documentation, not inspected
here) and reads the host path; ReScript binds the host server.
Ergo keeps Files lexical and puts the confinement in the capability, so a
test observes both halves (`FilesAsksBeneath`, `TestFSRootTreeAgreesWithHost`).

Files' performance comparison with `http.FileServer(http.FS(root.FS()))`
is pending in `wf10/g1-http/bench-pending.md`. Both use a held root; the
comparison must match buffering, empty-file headers and HEAD handling.
The benchmark harness is not built yet. No Files parity claim is made.

Prior art (source/documentation inspected, not measured): Mist 6.0.3 uses
`fn(Request) -> Response` and a required size at `read_body`; its buffered
adapter chooses a failure response. Wisp 2.2.2 exposes body-read helpers and
[`handle_head`](https://hexdocs.pm/wisp/2.2.2/wisp.html#handle_head).
Mist's [body API](https://hexdocs.pm/mist/6.0.3/mist.html#read_body)
reads lazily unless its buffered adapter is selected.
Plug 1.20.3 [passes a connection through functions](https://hexdocs.pm/plug/1.20.3/Plug.Conn.html)
and reads
bodies lazily. These run on BEAM processes; Ergo lowers to net/http and
owned Go task children. ReScript instead binds the host server through FFI;
its uncurried callbacks need no effect interpreter, but have no service
row or owned request task. MoonBit's task groups are the relevant ownership
comparison, not an HTTP implementation claim. Effect HttpServer supplies
the scoped-server intent; Ergo takes ordinary functions and checked rows
instead of runtime tags, Effect instructions and middleware composition.

## toml

`toml.Decode(c json.Codec[T], text string) T fails toml.Malformed | wired[T]`
reads TOML 1.0 through the same derived codec as `json`. `toml.Open(text,
maxBytes, maxDepth)` returns a `json.Reader` for `decode[T]`.
`toml.Encode[T wire](v T) string fails toml.Unrepresentable` writes v as a
TOML document (S-config): it encodes v with the derived JSON codec, then
`runtime/v1.TOMLFromJSON` writes that tree as TOML, so one encoder per type
serves both formats. The top value must be a record; a text or a key that
is not UTF-8 and a null (TOML has none) fail `Unrepresentable{Key,
Reason}`. Law `EncodeRoundTrips`: `Decode` reads back what `Encode`
writes.

1. The whole document is validated before a value is decoded. Syntax and
   limit failures are `Malformed{Line, Key, Reason}`: a 1-based source line,
   the dotted key being read, and the failed rule. Derived shape, refinement
   and `ParseX` failures retain the shared codec's typed rows and paths.
2. Field naming, unknown-field skipping, maps, slices, unions (an explicit
   `_tag` key) and refinements are the shared codec's rules. TOML has no
   null; absence is an omitted `json.Optional` field, not a new null value.
3. Integers are signed 64-bit; dates and times map to canonical strings
   (upper-case T/Z, fractions trimmed to nanosecond precision). Floats
   use float64 and json's `NaN`/`Infinity`/`-Infinity` strings. Input is UTF-8.
   Tables, arrays of tables, dotted and quoted keys, inline tables, and
   basic/literal/multiline strings follow TOML 1.0. Duplicate keys and
   redefinitions fail, not last-wins as JSON does.
4. MaxBytes bounds the input, and MaxDepth bounds nested values and key
   paths. Nonpositive limits mean no byte bound and depth 10000. Parsing
   keeps a tree then writes validated JSON for the existing Reader. This
   costs an extra tree/text pass; direct Reader events are a follow-up.
   No speed or parity claim is made.

Intent and prior art: Effect `ConfigProvider` reads external configuration
into typed values; Ergo uses the declared record and derivation, not Config
builders, Schema classes, service tags or tagged runtime error trees (W4,
W5, W6). Go has no standard TOML decoder; BurntSushi/toml parses tables
then decodes into Go values. Gleam `tom` decodes a TOML value with typed
decoder functions; Elixir `toml` returns maps and date/time structs;
ReScript uses host libraries. These are ordinary library calls lowered
to Erlang/JavaScript or BEAM, not compiler-known TOML operations. Ergo adds
no checker or lowering branch: its parser is a Go runtime body and its
decoder is the existing Reader derivation.

Acceptance: `runtime/v1.TestTOMLCorpus` reads the public toml-test 1.0
selection at `96fd0884`, with upstream license and byte-exact data in
`runtime/v1/testdata/toml-test`. `TestTOMLDecode` runs the generated-record
law through the public derived decoder; `TestTOMLFailuresNameLineAndKey`
checks the diagnostic source location. These are tests, not proofs.

## config

Std `config` (S-config) reads a program's configuration, typed, from
layered sources, and holds its secrets. The configuration is the
program's own record, read by its derived codec ([types.md,
Codecs](types.md#codecs)):

```ergo
type Settings { Limit int, Tag string, Remote string }

let spec = config.Spec{Prefix: "BOOKMARKS", Flags: config.FromFlags(v),
	Files: []config.File{config.File{Path: dirs.Config + "/config.toml", Format: config.TOML}},
	Defaults: config.DefaultsOf(Settings{Limit: 10, Tag: "", Remote: "https://bookmarks.example/api"})}
let s = try config.Load[Settings](spec)
```

1. **The capability.** `Config` is an operation type: `Lookup(name)
   Option[string]`, `Environ(prefix) []Variable` (sorted by name) and
   `ReadFile(path) Option[string] fails Unreadable` (`None` when nothing is
   there: a configuration file is optional). `config.NewHost()` (needs
   `Ambient`) holds an `os.Host` and an `fs.Host` and reads through their
   functions, so each host body has one Ergo owner; `config.NewMemory(env,
   files)` is the provider for tests. `Load`, `LoadSecret` and `UserDirs`
   say `needs Config`, and `main` binds `context { config.Config:
   config.NewHost() }`. Laws: `Config.EnvironAgreesWithLookup`,
   `Config.ReadIsRepeatable`.
2. **Layers.** `Load[T wire](s Spec) T fails Unreadable | Malformed |
   wired[T] needs Config` reads, highest first: the flags the command line
   gave (`FromFlags(v)` takes only flags that were typed, never a flag's
   default: `flag.GivenFlags`), the variables `Prefix_KEY` (none when the
   prefix is empty), each file of `Files` in order (a missing file is
   skipped), and `Defaults` (`DefaultsOf(value)`; `NoDefaults()`). The
   default is stated once, as a value of the record. Two objects merge key
   by key; any other value of a higher layer replaces the lower one whole,
   so a list is never concatenated. Law `HigherLayerWins`.
3. **Names.** A key is the field's wire name; a nested key joins names
   with ".". `FlagName("server.port")` is `server.port`, `FlagName("apiToken")`
   `api-token`; `EnvName("NOTES", "apiToken")` is `NOTES_API_TOKEN` and
   `EnvName("NOTES", "server.port")` `NOTES_SERVER__PORT`. `FlagKey` and
   `EnvKey` invert them (law `NamesInvert`). A key no field has is skipped,
   as the codec skips unknown fields, so one prefix serves several records.
4. **Texts.** A flag and a variable are text, read as the kind the field
   asks for: an int by `strconv.ParseInt(text, 0, 64)` as std `flag`
   reads one, a float by `ParseFloat`, a bool by `ParseBool`; a list field
   splits each text at ","; a union field reads a variant with no field by
   its name. A file's values are read as TOML or JSON give them (law
   `RoundTripsThroughFiles`: a record written by `json.Encode` or
   `toml.Encode` loads back as itself).
5. **Failures.** A key no layer has fails `WireMissingField`
   (`MissingKey(m)` is its dotted key; `Looked(spec, key)` lists the
   sources Load tried, and `Describe(source)` writes one: `flag --api-token`,
   `env NOTES_API_TOKEN`, `file c.toml`, `default`). A value of the wrong
   kind fails `WireWrongKind` whose `Path` names the key and the source,
   `limit (env BOOKMARKS_LIMIT)`, never the value. A file's syntax fails
   `Malformed{Path, Line, Key, Reason}`; a file that is there and cannot be
   read fails `Unreadable{Path, Reason}`. No failure holds a configured
   text, so a secret's failure cannot show the secret.
6. **Secrets.** `Redacted[T]` is a sealed operation type with one
   operation, `Reveal() T`; `NewRedacted(v)` is its only maker, and
   `LoadSecret[T](spec) Redacted[T]` reads a T and holds it. By
   construction, not at run time:
   - interpolation of a Redacted is refused (S108: an operation type has
     no text form);
   - encoding a value whose type reaches a Redacted, with `json.Encode`,
     `toml.Encode` or any derived codec, is refused at check time (S108:
     an operation type has no wire form, [types.md, Codecs](types.md#codecs)
     1). A program that must send a secret calls `Reveal` where a reader
     sees it;
   - a failure report writes the field as `<Redacted>`, the
     type's name ([types.md, Methods](types.md#methods) 6), and a law's
     counterexample writes the builder's call `config.NewRedacted[T](…)`;
   - the Go body (`runtime/v1/redacted.go`) keeps the value **behind a
     pointer field, never inline in the struct**, so Go's `fmt`, which
     prints a value it cannot call a method on field by field, can print
     only an address; its `String`, `GoString`, `Format`, `MarshalJSON`,
     `MarshalText` and `LogValue` write `<redacted>`. Go code reached by a
     foreign call writes no byte of the secret.
   Law `RevealIsTheValue`. A configuration record holds no Redacted (it
   would have no codec); read the secret with its own record and
   `LoadSecret`, and never from a flag, which the process list shows.
7. **Directories.** `UserDirs(app) Dirs fails NoHome needs Config` is the
   app's `Config`, `Cache` and `State` directories by the XDG Base
   Directory rules on Linux and macOS alike: `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/app`, else
   `$HOME/.config/app`, and so on; a variable that is unset, empty or not
   absolute is ignored. Go's `os.UserConfigDir` gives `~/Library/Application
   Support` on macOS; command-line programs there (git, gh, fish) read
   `~/.config`, and so does this.

**Effect intent.** `Config` and `ConfigProvider` describe the
configuration a program needs, read it from the environment, files and the
command line with defaults, and fail with the key and the reason;
`Redacted` keeps a token out of logs and encoded output. Not taken: the
`Config.string`/`Config.integer` builder per key, `Config.withDefault` and
`Config.nested` combinators (W1, W3, W7: the record, its codec and one
`Spec` say it), `ConfigProvider` as a `Layer` (W4, W13: the `Config`
capability and `context`), `ConfigError` trees with `_tag` (W5, W6: the
row and `catch`), and `Redacted`'s run-time `toString`/`toJSON` and its
module-level `WeakMap` (W19: the type has no text and no wire form, so the
checker refuses it).

**Prior art.** Go's `flag`, `os.Getenv` and `encoding/json` leave the
layering to the program; viper and koanf merge maps of `any` and decode
with `mapstructure` at run time. Rust's `figment` merges providers into one
`serde` value and names the source of a bad value, as `WireWrongKind`'s
path does; `secrecy::SecretBox` has no `Display`. Gleam has no std
configuration (`envoy` reads one variable; `glenvy` loads `.env` files);
Elixir's `Config` scripts evaluate keyword lists at boot and
`System.fetch_env!` raises; ReScript reads `process.env` through bindings.
None of these refuses a secret at compile time: each lowers to an ordinary
library call on the host. Ergo lowers `Load` to one key tree of the layers
and the shared Reader derivation, and `Redacted` to a Go pointer type.

**Laws and runners.** lower `TestStdConfigLawsRun` restates the six
laws of `config` as Ergo property tests (`Config`'s over a generated
`Memory`) and runs them with `--seed 1`; `TestTOMLDecode` runs
`toml.EncodeRoundTrips`. lower `TestRedactedRefusedAtCheck` holds, over
generated types that hold a Redacted behind records, unions and slices,
that `json.Encode`, `toml.Encode` and interpolation are refused and the
same type with a string is accepted; lower `TestRedactedNeverPrints`, over
a generated program, that no failure report holds a secret and each
counterexample writes the builder; runtime/v1 `TestRedactedNeverRenders`,
over generated Go values, that no `fmt` verb, `fmt.Errorf` chain,
`encoding/json` or `log/slog` handler writes any byte of the secret.
`examples/bookmarks` reads its settings and its token this way.

## Build-time assets

1. The predeclared operation `embedded[T](pattern)` is the whole initializer of an immutable, named package `let`. `T` is `string`, `[]byte`, or `embed.FS`. The pattern is a constant string relative to that package's folder. It follows Go's slash-separated glob and directory expansion rules; `all:` includes hidden files when expanding a directory. A scalar embeds exactly one file; a tree embeds all matched files. A path outside the package, a missing match, a symbolic link or an unsupported file name is S108 at the pattern. A checker without an asset loader refuses the operation; it never reads files implicitly.
2. `embed.FS` is a read-only provider of `fs.Files`: `ReadFile`, `ReadDir` and `Stat` keep fs's typed failures. Names use `io/fs` paths rooted at the declaring package. There is no writer or host authority. A consumer states `needs fs.Files`, and its context chooses embedded assets, `fs.Host` or `fstest.Memory`. `embed.NewEmpty()` is an empty read-only tree.
3. The loader captures each selected file's bytes as a build input, imported assets included. An asset-only edit changes the build revision. Lowering emits `//go:embed` plus asset files, not payload Go literals. Go compiles the bytes into the executable. A string is a direct Go embedded string; bytes keep the ordinary slice value rule; the tree is a Go `embed.FS` with its private emitted namespace removed by `io/fs.Sub`. Unused runtime facilities remain subject to Go's linker reachability.
4. Intent: ship help text and static assets in a standalone binary without a runtime disk dependency. Effect's `FileSystem` supplies the replaceable read boundary but has no compile-time embedding operation. Ergo does not copy its service tags or layer builders: operation types, rows and `context` express those contracts. Go's `//go:embed` is the target control. Gleam/ReScript use host asset tooling; Elixir's external resources track compile dependencies but release assets are usually separate files. No runtime speed claim follows from this design.
5. **Zone data** (S-tz; owner q107). A program asks for its binary to carry Go's copy of the zone database with one line in the package of `main`: `import go time/tzdata` ([interop.md](interop.md#checking), Checking 6; anywhere else it is S108). Lookup stays host-first, Go's rule: `time.LoadLocation` reads `ZONEINFO`, then the host's zone directories (`/usr/share/zoneinfo` and the others), then the carried data, then `$GOROOT/lib/time/zoneinfo.zip`. So a host with zone data gives its own answer, and a host with none (a scratch container, a minimal image) still finds every zone of Go's release. Cost (measured, go1.27.0 linux/amd64, `ergo build` of a program that loads one zone, unstripped): 6,081,401 bytes against 5,665,809 without the line, 415,592 bytes more (7.3%). Start time (inferred, not measured): the data is a constant string that Go registers at initialization, with no parse until a lookup reads it. A program that does not carry it pays nothing. Lower `TestZoneDataCarried` runs the program with and without the line in a user and mount namespace that hides every host source: only the carried one finds the zone. Replaces the `-tags timetzdata` build tag, which a build sets outside the source, where no reader of the program sees it.

## Std laws

HTTP's transport lifetime runs in `runtime/v1.TestHTTPConcurrentRequestsAndStop`
and `TestHTTPDisconnectAndDefects`; its buffered requests run against a map
model in `TestHTTPServerMatchesModel`. These are socket tests, not proofs.

The std states its guarantees as laws (owner, 2026-11-06; std-align S1a). The rule:

1. Each operation type of each std package, the prelude included, has at least one member law. A type that no law over its own operations can observe has instead one doc-comment line `// law-exempt: <reason>`, and the reason is not empty. A type never has both. `TestStdOperationTypesHaveLaws` finds every operation type from the checked packages (`std.Packages()` and the prelude), never from a written list, and holds the rule.
2. A family of pure functions states its algebra as top-level laws in its package (round trips, idempotence, order).
3. A law is checked, not run, until [#162](https://github.com/cevr/ergo/issues/162) runs laws ([types.md, Laws](types.md#laws) 10), and it adds no Go to any build (Laws 11; lower `TestStdLawsAddNoGo`). Until #162, the table below names, for each law, the Go test that runs it now. A runner restates the law over the std's providers with seeded cases. `#162` stands only for a law that no Go test can run, such as a law on a type that the std provides no provider of, and its state is `checked`; a law with a Go runner is `tested`.
4. Each law of std source has exactly one row. The Statement is the law's source text from its name to its closing brace, each run of white space made one space. `TestStdLawLedger` holds both, and that each runner is a `func Test…` that a runner package (`internal/sketch/lower`, `internal/sketch/std`, `runtime/v1`) declares and that runs its laws through `lawledger.Run` (`internal/lawledger`). A runner gives `lawledger.Run` each law it restates, with the statement it restates and the check that runs it. `lawledger.Run` holds that the runner restates exactly the rows that name it, each with the row's statement (so a law changed in source and here fails its runner until its restatement changes too), and runs each law as a subtest named after its key (`queue.CloseKeepsValues`). `TestStdLawsRun` (lower) restates every law whose runner it is as an Ergo test of the same name, and runs them with `--seed 1`, as `ergo test --seed 1` does.
5. **A Seq stops.** A `Seq` calls yield with each value and stops when yield returns false (prelude, `Seq`). `Seq` is a function type, so it cannot carry a member law, and Go's `iter.Seq` states the rule in prose only. So each exported std function and operation whose result is the prelude's `Seq` states it as a law named `<Producer>Stops`: with n the count of values a yield that always returns true gets, a yield that returns false at its k-th call (k of 1 or more) is called exactly min(k, n) times. A function is a top-level law of its package, and a function whose first parameter is a type of its package is named by that type and itself (io's `Lines(input Input)` states `InputLinesStops`); an operation is a member law of its type (`Queue.ValuesStops`, which also states that the values not taken stay in the queue). A top-level stop law drives every k from 1 to n+1 itself; the runner of a member law drives each k. A producer that no law can drive with generated values has instead one doc-comment line `// seq-exempt: <reason>`, with a reason that is not empty, and never both: today `io.Lines(Host)` and the operation `io.Stdin.Lines`, which read the host. `TestStdSeqProducersStateStop` (`internal/sketch/std`) finds every producer from the checked packages by its result type, never from a list, and holds the rule.

The exempt types and their reasons are in their doc comments: `Console` and `Ambient` have no read, and `Scheduler` has no clock to observe a sleep by. `Clock` moved to std `time` with `Now() time.Time` and the law `NowNeverFalls` (std-align S2b).

| `utf8` | `RuneCountAddsRune` | `RuneCountAddsRune(s string) { RuneCount(s+"é") == RuneCount(s)+1 }` | `TestStdTextLaws` | tested |
| `utf8` | `RuneCountAtMostLen` | `RuneCountAtMostLen(s string) { RuneCount(s) <= len(s) && RuneCount(s) >= 0 }` | `TestStdTextLaws` | tested |
| `utf8` | `RuneCountOfConcat` | `RuneCountOfConcat(a string, b string) { RuneCount(a+b) <= RuneCount(a)+RuneCount(b) }` | `TestStdTextLaws` | tested |
| `codec` | `BasicReplay` | `BasicReplay(s string, b bool, i int8, u uint8, f float64) { let e = NewEvents() encode(s, e) encode(b, e) encode(i, e) encode(u, e) encode(f, e) let r = NewReplay(Recorded(e)) let s2 = decode[string](r) catch { case _: return false } let b2 = decode[bool](r) catch { case _: return false } let i2 = decode[int8](r) catch { case _: return false } let u2 = decode[uint8](r) catch { case _: return false } let f2 = decode[float64](r) catch { case _: return false } s2 == s && b2 == b && i2 == i && u2 == u && f2 == f }` | `TestStdCodecLawsRun` | tested |
| `codec` | `EncodeNests` | `EncodeNests(v fleet) { let e = NewEvents() encode(v, e) let mut open = 0 let mut ok = true for ev in Recorded(e) { open = open + depth(ev) if open < 0 { ok = false } } ok && open == 0 }` | `TestStdCodecLawsRun` | tested |
| `codec` | `EventsReplay` | `EventsReplay(v fleet) { let back = RoundTrip(v) catch { case _: return false } back == v }` | `TestStdCodecLawsRun` | tested |
| `codec` | `GenericReplay` | `GenericReplay(vs []state) { let (back, first) = many(vs) catch { case _: return false } let want Option[state] = if len(vs) > 0 { Some{Value: vs[0]} } else { None } back == vs && first == want }` | `TestStdCodecLawsRun` | tested |
| `codec` | `MissingFieldFails` | `MissingFieldFails() { let empty = []Event{BeginObjectEvent{N: 0}, EndObjectEvent} let _ = decode[up](NewReplay(empty)) catch { case WireMissingField: return true case _: return false } false }` | `TestStdCodecLawsRun` | tested |
| `codec` | `UnknownTagFails` | `UnknownTagFails(name string) { if name == "up" { return true } if name == "down" { return true } let evs = []Event{BeginObjectEvent{N: 0}, TagEvent{Name: name}, EndObjectEvent} let _ = decode[state](NewReplay(evs)) catch { case WireUnknownTag: return true case _: return false } false }` | `TestStdCodecLawsRun` | tested |
| `json` | `DepthLimits` | `DepthLimits(k int) { let n = if k < 0 { -(k + 1) } else { k } let depth = n % 64 + 1 let _ = Open(nest(depth), 0, depth) catch { case _: return false } let _ = Open(nest(depth + 1), 0, depth) catch { case Malformed: return true } false }` | `TestStdJSONLawsRun` | tested |
| `json` | `FastAgreesWithWriter` | `FastAgreesWithWriter(v fleet) { let t = NewText() t.BeginList(3) encode(v, t) t.Int(2) encode(v, t) t.EndList() let w = NewText() let r Writer = w r.BeginList(3) encode(v, r) r.Int(2) encode(v, r) r.EndList() let h = NewHTMLText() encode(v, h) let g = NewHTMLText() let gw Writer = g encode(v, gw) t.Written() == w.Written() && h.Written() == g.Written() && Encode(codec(), v) == viaWriter(v) }` | `TestStdJSONLawsRun` | tested |
| `json` | `RoundTrips` | `RoundTrips(v fleet) { let back = Decode(codec(), Encode(codec(), v)) catch { case _: return false } back == v }` | `TestStdJSONLawsRun` | tested |
| `json` | `OptionalRoundTrips` | `OptionalRoundTrips(v Option[int]) { optionalRoundTrips(v) }` | `TestJSONFieldsAgree` | tested |
| `json` | `NullableRoundTrips` | `NullableRoundTrips(v Option[int]) { nullableRoundTrips(v) }` | `TestJSONFieldsAgree` | tested |
| `json` | `NestedRoundTrips` | `NestedRoundTrips(v Option[Option[int]]) { nestedRoundTrips(v) }` | `TestJSONFieldsAgree` | tested |
| `json` | `ValueRoundTrips` | `ValueRoundTrips(d doc) { valueRoundTrips(d) }` | `TestJSONValueAgrees` | tested |
| `json` | `TruncatedFails` | `TruncatedFails(v fleet, cut int) { let text = Encode(codec(), v) let n = if cut < 0 { -(cut + 1) } else { cut } let short = text[:n % len(text)] let _ = Decode(codec(), short) catch { case Malformed: return true case _: return false } false }` | `TestStdJSONLawsRun` | tested |

*Prior art:* Haskell states class laws beside the class and runs them with QuickCheck's `prop_` functions; Elixir's `StreamData` states properties beside `test`; MoonBit ships its core with property tests; Gleam and ReScript state none in source. Ergo states each law in the std source, where a reader of the std sees it, and keeps the Go test that runs it in one ledger until #162.

## Std strengths over Effect

HTTP takes `HttpServer`'s purpose: serve a function with its service row,
and close its listener and request tasks at the serving scope's end.
It does not take middleware builders, runtime service tags, or Effect
instruction values. Ordinary functions, rows and child tasks state this
contract directly. No HTTP-specific checker rule is added.

The std takes the behaviour and the guarantees of Effect's modules, not an Effect API shape that exists only because TypeScript has no construct for the job (owner, 2026-11-08; [decision](../decisions.md#owner-direction-2026-11-08)). Each such shape is a **workaround**, with an id:

- **W1** `Effect.gen` and `yield*`: plain statements, `try`, `catch` and the error row ([rows.md, Error rows](rows.md#error-rows)).
- **W2** `pipe`: the dot form `x.f(y)` ([types.md, Methods](types.md#methods)).
- **W3** dual data-first and data-last signatures: one data-first function.
- **W4** `Context.Service`, `Layer` and `Effect.provide`: an operation type with laws, a `needs` row, `context` and `with` ([rows.md, Capability rows](rows.md#capability-rows)).
- **W5** `Data.TaggedError`, `_tag` and `catchTag`: an error type in a row and a total `catch` ([rows.md, Catch](rows.md#catch)).
- **W6** `Match` and `Data.taggedEnum`: a union and an exhaustive `match` ([types.md, Unions](types.md#unions)).
- **W7** `Schema` classes: a declared type and its derived codec ([types.md, Codecs](types.md#codecs)).
- **W8** `Equal` and `Hash` traits: generated equality, `comparable` and Go's map hash.
- **W9** `Brand`: construction authority and refinements ([types.md, Zero values](types.md#zero-values-and-definite-initialisation) 6).
- **W10** `Option` and `Result` objects with combinators: the prelude union `Option[T]` and `match`.
- **W11** `Scope` values passed through the program: frame scopes and `scope.OnExit`.
- **W12** `Context.Reference`, `withSpan` and `annotateLogs` as fiber-local values: context values bound by `with` ([runtime.md, G1](runtime.md#g1-context-through-with)).
- **W13** `Config` and `ConfigProvider.fromEnv`: a capability that reads the process and a derived codec.
- **W14** every operation as an `Effect` value run by the fiber runtime: a direct Go call ([rows.md, Footprints](rows.md#footprints)).
- **W15** `Effect.forEach` and `Effect.all` with a `concurrency` option: a `for` loop, or `task.Go` for concurrent work.
- **W16** `Order` and `Equivalence` values passed by hand: the `ordered` and `comparable` constraints ([types.md, Generics](types.md#generics)).
- **W17** a command typed by inference from an object of flag values (`Flag`, `Command.make`): a declared type with a derived parser (S5a; not built).
- **W18** `Random` and `Clock` as references with a default: prelude and `time` capabilities bound by `context`.
- **W19** `Redacted` with a runtime `toString`: `config.Redacted[T]`, a sealed operation type that interpolation and every codec refuse at check time ([config](#config) 6).
- **W20** `Effect.timeout` that forks a fiber per call: `timeout.After(d) { … }`, an ordinary function whose row adds `Expired` ([runtime.md, Timeouts](runtime.md#timeouts)).

The table has one row per std package, the prelude included. The Effect module is a file of `packages/effect/src` at pin `460272d3`, the pin of `conformance/effect-cases.json`. The last column is the package's lowering against hand-written Go: a benchmark the repository declares, as `<package dir>.Benchmark<Name>`, with its last recorded result and where the result is kept; or `pending:` and the file that holds the command; or `none:` and the reason. A result is a measurement on a shared host unless the cell says otherwise; a speed claim needs a quiet-host run (common rule). `TestStdStrengthsTable` finds the packages from `std.Packages()` and the prelude, never from a written list, and fails for a package with no row, a row for no package, an empty cell, a workaround id outside W1 to W20, an Ergo construct with no spec link, and a benchmark that the named package's tests do not declare. It checks names, not truth: a reviewer reads each row against the package source.

| Package | Effect module | Workarounds not copied | Ergo construct | Lowering against hand Go |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

Quiet-host command: `wf10/g1-http/bench-pending.md`. CU5's matched redirect/policy harness is also pending. No parity claim yet. |

Hand-Go control: the same parser followed by encoding/json into an equivalent Go config record; generated decoder/control comparison pending in `wf10/cli-units/cu7/bench-pending.md`. Current tree plus JSON transcode costs an extra pass; direct Reader events are the follow-up. |

*Prior art:* ReScript makes pipe-first `->` a plain call and uncurries by default (W2, W3); Elixir's `|>` is a macro and `with` chains the happy path (W1, W2), and `@derive` generates protocol code (W7, W8); Gleam's `use` replaces callbacks with plain lines and its `Result` replaces exceptions (W1, W5), with no type classes (W16); Roc and MoonBit derive equality, hashing and codecs (W7, W8), and Roc gives each host effect as a platform capability (W4, W18); Elm has no derivation and writes decoders by hand, the shape W7 and W17 avoid; Koka's evidence passing makes a tail-resumptive handler a direct call, as Ergo's environment of capabilities does (W14).

## Packages by origin

- **Same as Go:** `strings`, `strconv`, `utf8`, `slices`, `maps`, `cmp`, `math`, `bits`, `csv` (Go's `encoding/csv`), `hex`, `sha256`, `crc32`, `time`, `rand`, `os`, `fs`, `net`, `sql`.
- **Same name, extended:**
  - `http`: client, handlers, routes, server limits;
  - `json`: `Codec`, `Decode`, `Encode`, `ParseX`/`Wire`;
  - `base64`: Go's two alphabets as the union `Encoding`;
  - `sync`: `Ref`, `TxRef`, `Atomically`, `Latch`;
  - `testing`: `T`, `VirtualClock`, `Discard`, `Idle`.
- **New:** `tasks`, `schedule` (the schedule algebra, with laws), `retry`, `timeout`, `trace`, `log`, `metric`, `batch`, `actor` (the live machine runner: `StartMachine`, `Send`, `Ask`, `State`, `Stop`, `Changes`, `Dropped`, `Feed`, `Can`), `machines` (the runs of a machine value: the pure `Run` and `From`, `RunTimed` with state timers on the context's `time.Clock` and `Scheduler`, `Start` for a machine with an input, `Next`, `Initial`, `Can`), `html`.

## Schedule

[#179](https://github.com/cevr/ergo/issues/179). Package `schedule` says when to run work again and when to stop: the policy of a retry, a repeat, or a restart budget. `tasks.Retry` and `tasks.Repeat` run work by one.

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Schedule = …` | A policy. Its variants are unexported, so only the functions below make one. |
| `type Next = Continue \| Stop`, `type Continue { After duration }`, `type Stop {}` | A decision: run again after `After`, or stop. |
| `func Spaced(d duration) Schedule`, `func Forever() Schedule` | Continue forever, after `d` or with no delay. |
| `func Exponential(base duration, factor float64, cap duration) Schedule` | Continue forever; after run `n` the delay is `base × factor^(n−1)`, at most `cap`. |
| `func Recurs(n int) Schedule` | Continue after runs 1 to `n`, then stop. |
| `func UpTo(s Schedule, limit duration) Schedule` | `s`, but stop where a run would start more than `limit` after the first run began. |
| `func Jitter(s Schedule, low float64, high float64, seed uint64) Schedule` | `s`, each delay times a factor in `[low, high)` drawn from `seed` and the run count. |
| `func And(a Schedule, b Schedule) Schedule`, `func Or(a Schedule, b Schedule) Schedule` | Continue while both continue, after the longer delay; continue while either continues, after the shorter delay of those that continue. |
| `func Decide(s Schedule, runs int, elapsed duration) Next` | The decision after `runs` runs (1 for the first), `elapsed` after the first run began. |
| `func Retry[T, E, R](s Schedule, block func() T fails E needs R) T fails E needs R \| Scheduler \| time.Clock` (package `tasks`) | Run `block` until it gives a value; after a failure, `Decide` continues or stops, and on Stop `Retry` fails with that run's failure. |
| `func Repeat[T, E, R](s Schedule, block func() T fails E needs R) T fails E needs R \| Scheduler \| time.Clock` (package `tasks`) | Run `block`, and again while `Decide` continues; give the last value. A failure ends `Repeat`. |

1. **Purpose** (Effect `Schedule`). A program retries a call that fails for a while (a network, a lock), polls, or limits how often a supervisor restarts a worker. It needs one value that says how long to wait and when to give up, that it can test without waiting.
2. **A pure value.** A `Schedule` is data: a tree of the combinators above. `Decide` is a pure function of the schedule, the run count and the elapsed time, so a test folds it with no clock, and a law states each combinator. The schedule keeps no state between decisions: the run count and the elapsed time are the whole input, and the jitter draw is SplitMix64 of `seed + runs` (inferred: equal to Effect's state for every combinator here, since none of them reads its last output).
3. **One clock.** `Retry` and `Repeat` read the elapsed time from the context's `time.Clock` and sleep on its `Scheduler`, the clock of [runtime.md, Machines and actors](runtime.md#machines-and-actors) 9.6. On a `testing.VirtualClock` a retry with an hour of backoff runs at once (laws `RetryFollowsDecide`, `RepeatFollowsDecide`).
4. **Totality.** Each constructor takes every argument: a duration below zero is zero (as Go's `time.Sleep` reads it), an exponential factor below 1 (or NaN) is 1, a `Recurs` count below zero is zero, and each jitter bound is moved into `[0, 1000]` with `high` at least `low`. Each part of a jittered delay saturates at 4.6e18 ns, so their sum does not overflow.
5. **UpTo never starts late.** Effect's `upTo(d)` stops once the elapsed time passes `d`, so its last delay may start a run after `d`. `UpTo(s, limit)` stops where the delay would start the run after `limit` (law `UpToNeverPassesLimit`).
6. **Jitter is seeded.** The draw comes from the seed in the value, not from a `Random` capability, so `Retry` needs no `Random` and a test replays it. To keep many clients apart, each draws its seed once from `Random`. The jittered delay is the sum of two scaled durations, not `d × (low + span × u)`: no float product is added to, so no Go target can fuse a multiply-add and round another way (the model test compares exact nanoseconds).
7. **No predicate over the failure.** In `Retry[T, E, R]`, `E` is an error row, not a type ([types.md, Generics](types.md#generics) 1), so no function can take the failure as an argument, and `Retry` has no `while` predicate. A failure that must not be retried is caught in `block` and given as a value of its result.
8. **Re-raise.** `Retry`'s body catches `block`'s failure with `case err:` and, on Stop, returns `err`: a catch binding over a row parameter is a failure when returned ([rows.md, Catch](rows.md#catch) 7).
9. **Effect parts not taken.** `Schedule<Out, In, R>`'s three type parameters, its outputs and its input pipelining (`Schedule.passthrough`, `andThen`, `collectAllOutputs`, `whileInput`): no combinator here reads an input or an output, and a schedule's meaning is a function of the run count and the time (see 7 for the failure). Its fiber-backed step (W14) and `Schedule.driver` (an effect-returning state machine): `Decide` is a pure call. `pipe` and dual APIs (W2, W3): `s.And(t).UpTo(d)` by the dot form. Cron and calendar schedules (`Schedule.cron`, `windowed`) are not built.
10. **Laws.** `RecursStopsAfter`, `UpToNeverPassesLimit`, `ExponentialUnderCap`, `JitterInBounds`, `AndIsMaxOrIsMin`, and `tasks`'s `RetryFollowsDecide` and `RepeatFollowsDecide` ([Std laws](#std-laws)); lower `TestStdScheduleLawsRun` runs them on generated arguments. Lower `TestScheduleAgreesWithModel` compares `Decide`, lowered, with a Go reference model on generated schedules (every combinator, every clamp, both ends of each decision; `ERGO_SCHEDULE_SEED` replays a seed).

*Prior art:* Effect's `Schedule` (the reference for the intent); Go has no std backoff, and `cenkalti/backoff` keeps a mutable `ExponentialBackOff` with a randomization factor and a `MaxElapsedTime`; Erlang/OTP supervisors count restarts in a window (`intensity`, `period`); Elixir's `Retry` library composes delay streams (`exponential_backoff |> cap |> expiry`), a lazy list of delays, close to `Decide` folded over the runs; Gleam and ReScript have no schedule in their std.

## Tasks

[#207](https://github.com/cevr/ergo/issues/207). `tasks.Set` is G4 ([runtime.md](runtime.md#g4-tasksset)).

| Declaration | Meaning | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `func Transform[A, B, E, R](xs []A, limit int, f escaping func(x A) B fails E needs R) []B fails E needs R` | `f` of each element, each call on a task of its own, at most `limit` at once, the results in the order of `xs` (batch 12, C2; built). `f` is escaping: its calls run at the same time ([types.md](types.md#function-values), Function values 2; ESC1). | `TasksTransform(span Site, t *rt.Task, rel Releaser[[]B], xs []A, limit int, f func(*rt.Task, A) (B, error)) ([]B, error)` (task.go), over `rt.MapSpan` (rt tasks.go) |
| `func Race[T, E, R](blocks []func() T fails E needs R) T fails E needs R` | Each block on a task of its own; the first to end decides, and the rest are stopped and waited for (batch 12, C4; built). | `TasksRace(span Site, t *rt.Task, rel Releaser[T], blocks []func(*rt.Task) (T, error)) (T, error)` (task.go), over `rt.RaceSpan` (rt tasks.go) |
| `law TransformEqualsLoop(xs []int, k int)`, `law TransformKeepsOrder(xs []int, k int)`, `law LimitHolds(xs []int, k int)`, `law EarliestWins(ds []int)`, `law LosersStopped(n int)` | The laws ([Std laws](#std-laws)). | `TestTasksLaws` (runtime/v1) |

1. **Name.** The function is the concurrent form of `slices.Transform` and has its name: `Map` is the prelude's type, which no package redeclares ([#196](https://github.com/cevr/ergo/issues/196); S108).
2. **Limit.** At most `limit` calls of `f` run at once: a call starts only while fewer run, and when one ends the next element starts. A `limit` below 1 is a defect of the caller, and no call starts.
3. **Order.** The result holds `f(xs[i])` at index `i`, whatever order the calls end in.
4. **Fail fast.** `Transform` waits for whichever call ends first. The first failure it sees ends it: no call starts after it, every call still running is stopped, and all are waited for before `Transform` fails with that failure. A defect in a call, or in the stop of another, is raised in the caller, as `Task.Wait` raises it.
5. **Structured.** Every task `Transform` starts has ended when it returns, on every path. A stop of the calling task stops every call, and `Transform` raises the interruption. The calls share `f`'s providers, which the caller's row binds.
6. **Virtual time.** The caller's wait is one the VirtualClock sees ([Testing](#testing) 2), so a `Transform` over sleeping calls runs in virtual time.

`TestTransformModel` (runtime/v1) runs 300 generated cases on a VirtualClock against a discrete-event model: the results or the failure, the elements started, the most calls at once and the instant `Transform` returns. *Prior art:* Go's `errgroup.Group` with `SetLimit` (the first error cancels the group's context; `Wait` waits for all); Effect's `Effect.forEach(xs, f, { concurrency })`, fail-fast with interruption of the rest; Elixir's `Task.async_stream` with `max_concurrency` and `ordered: true`; Gleam and ReScript have no bounded parallel map in their std (Gleam's OTP `task` awaits one by one). *Effect workarounds replaced:* the `{ concurrency }` options object of one combinator for sequential and concurrent loops (W15) becomes a plain `for` and `Transform`; `Effect.gen` and `yield*` around `f` (W1) become the plain call.

`Race` (Effect's `raceAll`; Go's `select` over result channels with a cancel of the losers):

7. **The first to end decides.** `Race` waits for whichever block ends first. Its value is `Race`'s value, and its failure is `Race`'s failure. Every other block is stopped, and `Race` returns only after each has ended, its exit actions included. A loser's failure is dropped (laws R3, R6); a defect in any block is raised in the caller.
