The host keeps a stack: each `SetMode` saves the state before it, and `Restore` sets back the latest saved state. A program calls the block forms; `SetMode` and `Restore` are the provider's halves.
2. **Keys.** `ReadKey` reads through `io`'s one byte buffer of standard input. A key is one byte, one UTF-8 rune, or an escape sequence (ESC `[` or ESC `O`, parameters, a final byte). An ESC with nothing after it in the bytes read is `Escape`, since a terminal writes a sequence in one write. A carriage return and a line feed are `Return`; delete and backspace are `Backspace`. A stop of the reading task ends the wait; the bytes read stay for the next read.
3. **Laws.** `Terminal` is law-exempt: no law over its operations can read the host's state. `RawRestores`, `NestedRestoresInOrder`, `ScriptPlaysKeys` and `DecodeNamesControls` hold the scope and the decoding over `Script`. Lower `TestTermModesRestore` is the model of the host: a built program on a pseudo-terminal runs generated sequences of raw, no-echo, read, leave and stop; at each read the terminal's state is the model's, and after the program the state is the state before it.
4. **Go bodies.** `v1.TermEnterRaw`, `v1.TermEnterNoEcho`, `v1.TermLeave`, `v1.TermReadKey` and `v1.TermGetSize` (term.go). The ioctls go through one `unsafe` function, `ioctl` in term_unix.go, with its invariant in its comment: the FFI edge of the 2026-11-02 rule, since Go's standard library has no safe form.

*Prior art:* Go's `golang.org/x/term` (`MakeRaw`, `Restore`, `GetSize`) does the same ioctls and leaves the restore to the caller's `defer`; Elixir's `IO.gets` reads a line, and OTP 26 adds a raw shell mode (`shell:start_interactive({noshell, raw})`) that the caller sets back; Gleam's standard library has no terminal module (`gleam_erlang` neither); Roc basic-cli's `Tty` has `enable_raw_mode!` and `disable_raw_mode!` with no pairing. Each lowers to its host's call with no scope; Ergo's block form lowers to one `OnExit` registration on the frame scope.

### prompt

Std `prompt` asks a person at a terminal for a line of text, a secret, one choice, several choices or a yes (CU3). Effect's cli `Prompt` (`text`, `password`, `select`, `multiSelect`, `confirm`) is the reference for intent: ask a person for a typed answer, and let a program that runs with no person see that it cannot. Ergo takes that purpose. It does not take Effect's `Prompt` values run by a runtime (W14), the `Terminal` service tag (W4) or the tagged `QuitException` (W5). Each prompt is a machine declaration over `term.Key` with a pure frame, and the function that runs it has its failures in its row.

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `func Line(message string, fallback string) string` | A line of text; Return on an empty line answers `fallback`. |
| `func Secret(message string) string` | A line of text whose frame shows one `*` per character. |
| `func Select(message string, options []string) int` | One option's index. |
| `func MultiSelect(message string, options []string) []int` | The marked options' indexes, ascending; a space marks or unmarks. |
| `func Confirm(message string, fallback bool) bool` | y or n; Return answers `fallback`. |
| `type NotInteractive {}`, `type Cancelled {}` | Every function above `fails NotInteractive \| Cancelled \| io.ReadFailed needs term.Terminal \| io.Stderr`. |
| `type Typer`, `Picker`, `Marker`, `Asker` (machines); `TypingStep`, `PickingStep`, `MarkingStep`, `AskingStep`; `TypingFrame`, `PickingFrame`, `MarkingFrame`, `AskingFrame` | The machines, their steps and their frames. Each machine's input is its question (`Text`, `Choice`, `Question`), and its output is `Option` of the answer: `None` is a cancel. |
| `func Ask[S, O, F, R, W](m Machine[S, term.Key, Option[O], F, R, W], frame func(s S) string) O` | Runs a prompt machine: raw mode (`term.Raw`), one key at a time, each new state's frame drawn over the last on `io.Stderr` (`Redraw`). |
| `func KeyOf(code int) term.Key` | A key of a code, for laws and properties over generated keys. |

1. **Not interactive is a failure.** On a standard input that is not a terminal, the host's `term.Raw` fails `term.NotTerminal`, and `Ask` fails `NotInteractive`. A caller decides what a run with no person does. Escape, Ctrl-C and Ctrl-D fail `Cancelled`.
2. **Frames are pure.** A frame is a function of the state. `Ask` writes the first frame, then for each new state a carriage return, a cursor-up per line of the last frame, an erase to the end of the screen and the new frame. Frames go to standard error, so standard output stays the program's answer.
3. **Laws and models.** `SelectStaysInRange`, `SecretFrameHidesText`, `SecretFramesHideTyping`, `MarkedAnswerIsSorted` and `ReturnAnswersTheDefault` are in [Std laws](#std-laws). Lower `TestPromptLaws` runs them. It also holds each machine to a reference model of its own over generated keys, and holds `Ask` on a `term.Script`: the mode is restored, a secret's drawn text equals that of as many x's, a terminal that is not one fails `NotInteractive`, and Escape fails `Cancelled`.
4. A std package may declare a machine: it lowers as a user package's machine does (lower `std.go`, `naming.go`).

*Prior art:* Charm's `huh` (Go) runs each field as a Bubble Tea model, a state machine with a view; Elixir's `Owl.IO` (`select`, `multiselect`, `confirm`) reads a line per answer; Gleam's `survey` reads lines with `erlang:get_line`; ReScript uses host libraries (Node's `readline`). Each lowers to its host's reads. Ergo's prompt lowers to a Go loop over one step function, with no runtime value per key.

### flag

Std `flag` reads a command line (std-align S1d). It follows Go's `flag` for the syntax, the names and the help layout, and Effect's `@effect/cli` for typed commands, required flags, choices, positional arguments and the closed union of failures. Two rules follow `@effect/cli` and not Go (owner, 2026-11-06, Q7): a flag may follow a positional argument until a bare `--`, and help goes to standard output with status 0. It is Ergo over `strings`, `strconv` and `time`, with no runtime body.

```go
import flag
import io
import os

let Json = flag.Bool("json", false, "print JSON")
let Sort = flag.Choice("sort", "date", []string{"date", "name"}, "the order")
let Files = flag.Remaining("files", "the files")

func run(args []string) fails os.Exit needs Console | io.Stdout {
	let set = flag.Set{Name: "list", Usage: "", Flags: []flag.Flag{Json, Sort}, Args: []flag.ArgSpec{Files}}
	let v = flag.Parse(set, args) catch {
	case err:
		try flag.Exit(err, flag.Usage(set))
		return
	}
	Console.Println("\(Json.Get(v)) \(Sort.Get(v)) \(len(flag.Rest(v)))")
}
```

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type StringFlag`, `IntFlag`, `BoolFlag`, `ChoiceFlag`, `DurationFlag` (unexported fields); `type Flag = StringFlag \| IntFlag \| BoolFlag \| ChoiceFlag \| DurationFlag` | Flag handles; only package `flag` builds one. |
| `String(name, def, usage string) StringFlag`, `Int(name string, def int, usage string) IntFlag`, `Bool(name string, def bool, usage string) BoolFlag`, `Duration(name string, def duration, usage string) DurationFlag` | Go's constructors, in Go's argument order. A `Duration` value is read by `time.ParseDuration` (std-duration); `Defaults` names its kind `duration` and writes a default that is not `0ns` with `time.DurationText`, as Go's `PrintDefaults` does. |
| `RequiredString(name, usage string) StringFlag`, `RequiredInt(name, usage string) IntFlag`, `Choice(name, def string, choices []string, usage string) ChoiceFlag` | Effect's additions: a flag with no default, and a flag whose value is one of `choices`. |
| `type ArgSpec` (unexported fields); `Arg(name, usage string)`, `OptionalArg(name, usage string)`, `Remaining(name, usage string)` | Positional arguments: one that must be given, one that may be absent, and the rest (last). |
| `type Set { Name string, Usage string, Flags []Flag, Args []ArgSpec }` | A command line. |
| `func Parse(s Set, args []string) Values fails Help \| Unknown \| NeedsValue \| Invalid \| Missing \| Extra` | Reads `args`. |
| `Get(f StringFlag, v Values) string`, `Get(f IntFlag, v Values) int`, `Get(f BoolFlag, v Values) bool`, `Get(f ChoiceFlag, v Values) string`, `Get(f DurationFlag, v Values) duration`, `Get(a ArgSpec, v Values) Option[string]`, `Rest(v Values) []string` | Typed reads, called as `f.Get(v)`. |
| `Usage(s Set) string`, `Defaults(flags []Flag) string` | The help, and Go's `PrintDefaults` text. |
| `type Command[C, E] { Name string, Usage string, Set Set, Build func(v Values) C fails E \| Help \| … \| UnknownCommand, Commands []Command[C, E] }`; `ParseCommand[C, E](name, usage string, commands []Command[C, E], args []string) C fails E \| Help \| … \| UnknownCommand`; `CommandUsage[C, E](name, usage string, commands []Command[C, E]) string` | A command tree that parses into the program's own `C`. A leaf declares `Commands: []Command[C, E]{}` and uses `Set` and `Build`. A group dispatches to `Commands`; `Group[C, E](name, usage, children)` builds one without a dummy `Build`. A group requires a child. A child named `""` is its level's own leaf (gap-group): `tool auth` runs it and `tool auth whoami` runs the child `whoami`, as Cobra's `Run` on a parent and `@effect/cli`'s handler on a command `withSubcommands` do. Flags belong to leaves, not inherited global sets. `Build` may fail with the program's own failures `E`, which reach the caller unchanged at every depth. A command whose `Build` fails nothing of its own is written `Command[C]` ([types.md, Generics](types.md#generics) 7). |
| `WithAlias(f, short)` for each handle kind | Declares one ASCII letter or digit as the short name. It replaces any earlier alias. `-n=5`, `-n 5` and `--number=5` read the same handle. Short names are not clustered. An invalid alias or a spelling declared twice in one set is a declaration defect. |
| `OptionalString(name, usage) OptionalStringFlag`, `OptionalInt(name, usage) OptionalIntFlag` | `Get` returns `Option[string]` or `Option[int]`: absence is `None`, while an explicitly given empty text or zero is `Some`. The last occurrence wins. |
| `f.Repeated()` for each scalar handle kind | A typed handle whose `Get` is a list of all occurrences, in input order. Absence gives an empty list; the scalar's default and required marker are not used. It supports strings, ints, switches, durations and choices. Like overloaded `Get`, it is called by the method spelling, so the receiver type selects the overload. |
| `Root[C, E] { Name string, Usage string, Commands []Command[C, E], Version Option[string] }`; `ParseRoot(root, args)`; `RootUsage(root)` | A declared root. `Some(version)` enables `--version` in the first argument and adds it to root help. It yields `Version{Text: version}` in the failure row, handled beside `Help`; it does not change a leaf's `Build` row. `None` leaves the built-in undeclared. |
| `RenderCommand(path []string, v Values) []string` | Prefixes a leaf's canonical `Render` with its command path. The caller keeps that path with its parsed command data; a generic `Build` need not have an inverse. |
| `type Help { Text string }`, `Unknown { Name string }`, `NeedsValue { Name string }`, `Invalid { Name string, Text string, Reason string }`, `Missing { Name string }`, `Extra { Text string }`, `UnknownCommand { Name string, Known []string, Group []string }`; `type Failure = Help \| … \| UnknownCommand` | The failures. |
| `Describe(f Failure) string`; `Exit(f Failure, usage string) fails os.Exit needs io.Stdout` | A failure's text, and its end: `Help` prints its text to standard output and returns (status 0); any other failure fails `os.Exit{Code: 2}` with its text and `usage`, which the entry writes to standard error. |
| `Render(v Values) []string`, `Equal(a Values, b Values) bool` | A command line that parses to `v` again, and the equality of two results: the same flags and argument specs, and the same value for each read (`Equal` reads each value with its handle, not through `Render`). |

1. **Syntax** (Go's `flag.FlagSet.Parse`). An argument is a flag when it starts with `-` and is longer than `-`. `-name` and `--name` are one flag. A value follows `=` or, for a flag that is not a switch, is the next argument, whatever it is (`-s --` gives `s` the value `--`). A switch takes only `-name` or `-name=value`, with `strconv.ParseBool`'s values. `---x`, `-=x` and `--=x` are `Invalid` with `Name` empty ("bad flag syntax"). `-` alone and the empty string are positional. An `Int` value is read by `strconv.ParseInt(s, 0, 64)`, so `0x10`, `0o17` and `1_000` are read as Go reads them. A scalar or optional handle keeps its last occurrence; a `Repeated` handle keeps every occurrence.
2. **Position** (owner, Q7). Flags and positional arguments may interleave; a bare `--` ends the flags, and every argument after it is positional. A value that starts with `-` is given as `--name=value`. Go stops at the first positional argument; `@effect/cli` does not.
3. **Help.** `-h`, `-help` and `--help` (also with `=value`) fail `Help` with `Usage(s)` when the set declares no flag of that name, as in Go; a set that declares `h` reads `-h` as its flag. Help wins over every other failure (Q7, as `@effect/cli`'s `Command.runWith` reads its global `--help` from every argument before it reports a parse error): a help word anywhere before `--`, and not the value of a flag that takes one, fails `Help`, whatever comes before or after it (`-zz --help` is help). In `ParseCommand`, when the first argument names no command, a help word anywhere before `--` fails `Help` with `CommandUsage`; after a command name, the command's `Parse` reads it. `Exit` prints `Help`'s text to standard output and returns, so `main` ends with status 0 (Q7; Go prints usage to standard error).
4. **Order of checks.** First help (3). Then the arguments are read in order, and the first failure ends the parse. After the flags, each required flag must be given (`Missing`), then the positional arguments go to `s.Args` in order: an `Arg` takes one or fails `Missing`, an `OptionalArg` takes one if any is left, `Remaining` takes the rest. A positional argument left over fails `Extra`.
5. **Help text.** `Usage(s)` is `Usage of NAME:` (`Usage:` with no name), `s.Usage` on its own line when it is not empty, `Defaults(s.Flags)`, and an `Arguments:` block in declaration order (`name`, `[name]` or `name...`). `Defaults` is Go's `PrintDefaults` byte for byte for `String`, `Int` and `Bool`: sorted by name, the switch of a one-byte name on one line, a back-quoted word of the usage as the value's name, and a default that is not the zero value; a `Choice`'s value name is its choices joined by `|`, and a required flag ends with ` (required)`. The text of each failure is Go's where Go has one, with one difference: an invalid switch value reads `invalid value "x" for flag -b: parse error`, where Go writes `invalid boolean value`.
6. **Handles.** `Get` reads by canonical handle name, and first checks that the handle is the set's flag of that name: the same kind, cardinality, alias, default, usage and choices. A handle of another set, or of another kind or default under the name of one of the set's flags, is a defect, whether the flag was given or not (`TestFlagForeignHandle`). `Parse` checks that every long or short spelling denotes one handle, before help or input validation. Repeated and optional handles retain the long name in storage; `Render` writes only long names, preserving absence and every repeated value.
7. **Laws** ([Std laws](#std-laws)): `RenderParseRoundTrip`, `IndependentFlagsCommute`, `LastWins`, `DoubleDashEndsFlags`, `ShortAndLongAgree`, `DefaultWhenAbsent`, `UnknownNamesTheFlag`, `HelpNamesEveryFlag`, `EachCommandReaches`, `BuildFailureReaches` (a `Build`'s own failure reaches the caller as it is, with its value) and `FlagCrossesPositional` (Q7). lower `TestFlagLaws` restates them as property tests. lower `TestFlagMatchesGo` checks the parse on 3,500 seeded argument lists (500 of them with a help word put in among other tokens): against Go's `flag` where every flag comes before the first positional argument and no help follows a failure, and against a written model of Go's `parseOne` with the Q7 rules (interleaving, and `@effect/cli`'s help precedence) on every list. `RenderParseRoundTrip` compares every read (each flag's `Get`, an `Arg`, an `OptionalArg` and `Rest`) of the result and of its re-parse, not `Equal`.
8. **Cost.** `Values` holds a `Map` of a union of the value kinds, so each value is boxed until a closed union of records lowers without boxing (X1). The `Set` is a value built at each call; a constant `Set` could lower `Parse` to a `switch` on the flag name (#243).
9. **Trees (CU1).** Dispatch consumes one command name per level and uses the complete path as the leaf's help name. Group help lists all descendant relative paths. Trees have no special compiler rule. **A level's own leaf** (gap-group) is its child named `""`; a level holds at most one (the first counts), and it has no children (a defect otherwise). When the first argument names no child: a help word before `--`, read with the leaf's flags, fails `Help` with the level's `CommandUsage`; else the own leaf parses every argument when none follows, the first is a flag or `--`, or the leaf takes positional arguments; else `UnknownCommand`. So a typo after a group whose leaf takes no positional argument is still `UnknownCommand`, as Cobra reports "unknown command" for a parent whose `Args` is `NoArgs`. `UnknownCommand.Group` is the path of group names from the root to the level (`["auth"]` for `tool auth bogus`, empty at the root), its `Known` leaves out the own leaf, and `Describe` adds " of auth" for a nested level. `CommandUsage` lists the own leaf at its level's path (the program's name at the top) beside the children. `Render` writes every flag and then `--` before the positional arguments, so a rendered own-leaf line never starts with a child's name. `RenderParseTreeRoundTrip` (to a named leaf or to a level's own leaf), `DeclaredShortAndLongAgree`, `AbsentIsNone`, `RepeatedKeepsOrder`, `HelpNamesEveryCommand` (each own leaf included) and `UnknownCommandNamesItsGroup` run in `TestFlagTreeLaws`. *Prior art:* Cobra runs a parent's `Run` when no subcommand matches and its `Args` accept the words; `@effect/cli` runs a parent's handler when no subcommand follows; Elixir's `OptionParser` and Gleam's `glint` route by path, and glint gives every path node its own `run`. `TestFlagTreesAgree` generates trees of depth 1 to 3, where each level may have an own leaf, with or without positional arguments, and compares distinct leaf values, all repeated scalar kinds, parse failures, typed `Build` failures and the root version with an independent Go parser. Replay: `ERGO_FLAG_TREE_SEED=31 go test -run '^TestFlagTreesAgree$' ./internal/sketch/lower`. Measured speed for these additions is not claimed; the hand-Go control is a recursive command-name switch plus Go's scalar readers and append-only occurrence slices. The earlier scalar benchmark above is historical, not a measurement of CU1.

*Prior art:* Go's `flag` supplies scalar readers and help; Cobra adds command trees and shorthand names. Effect `cli` supplies typed parsing, optional flags, repeated occurrences and subcommands. Its intent is to turn untrusted argument text into typed application commands; Ergo uses records, unions, `Option` and a `Build` failure row instead of `Command.make` inference, tagged validation errors or Effect generator/pipe wrappers. Gleam `glint` and `argv` use typed parser values and `Result`, lowered to ordinary Erlang or JavaScript calls; Elixir `OptionParser` uses `aliases` and `:keep` to retain repeats, executing as BEAM library code; ReScript uses host parsers, with ordinary uncurried JavaScript calls. MoonBit uses `Option` and host argument access. Ergo takes the guarantees, not a framework's API shape. Type-derived commands remain S-cli's later part.

### exec

Std `exec` runs programs of the host (std-align S3c, G8). It follows Go's `os/exec` for its names and its process model, and Effect's `ChildProcess` (`unstable/process`) for what a run gives back: the exit status and the output, with a stop of the caller that ends the child.

```go
import exec

func branch() string needs exec.Runner {
	let r = exec.Runner.Run(exec.New("git", []string{"branch", "--show-current"})) catch {
	case exec.NotFound:
		return "no git"
	case exec.Denied, exec.Failed:
		return "git did not start"
	}
	if r.Status != 0 {
		return "not a repository"
	}
	r.Stdout
}

func main() needs Console | Ambient {
	with context { exec.Runner: exec.NewHost() } { Console.Println(branch()) }
}
```

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Command { Name string, Args []string, Env Map[string, string], Dir string, Stdin string }`; `func New(name string, args []string) Command`; `WithEnv(c, key, value)`, `WithDir(c, dir)`, `WithStdin(c, text)`; `func Key(c Command) string` | One program to run, and the builder of changed copies. |
| `type Result { Status int, Stdout string, Stderr string }` | What a program that ran gives back. |
| `type Runner { Run(c Command) Result fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed, law NotFoundNamesTheProgram(name string) }` | The capability. |
| `type Host { live hostOnly }`; `func NewHost() Host needs Ambient`; `func Run(_ Host, c Command) …` | The host's provider and its one constructor ([Host packages](#host-packages)). |
| `type Script { Results Map[string, Result] }`; `func Run(s Script, c Command) …`; `func Start(s Script, c Command, st Streams) …` | The test provider: it runs nothing. |
| `Runner.Start(c Command, s Streams) Running fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed`, `law StartAgreesWithRun(name string)` | Start a program that runs while its output is read (CU4). |
| `type Sink = Inherit \| Discard \| ToFile{Path string, Append bool} \| ToCaller`; `type Streams { Stdout Sink, Stderr Sink }` | Where each stream of a started program goes. |
| `type Running { Lines() Seq[Line], Chunks() Seq[Chunk], Wait() int, Stop() int takes, Close() }`; `type Line { Text string, Stderr bool }`; `type Chunk { Text string, Stderr bool }`; `func Split(text string) []string` | A started program, a resource; its lines; its bytes as read; the line rule. |
| `func WaitFor(p Running, d duration) int fails timeout.Expired needs Scheduler`, `law WaitForEndedGivesStatus(status int, d int64)` | Wait for the end with a deadline; Expired leaves the program running (S-tz). |
| `Command.Grace duration`; `WithGrace(c, d)` | The time between SIGTERM and SIGKILL when a stop, a timeout or a release ends the program; 0 (New's) sends SIGKILL at once. |
| `type NotFound { Name string }`; `type Denied { Name string }`; `type Failed { Name string, Reason string }` | The failures. |

1. `Name` is a program on the `PATH`, or a path when it holds a `/`, as for Go's `exec.Command`. `Args` are the arguments after the name. `Env` holds entries set over the host process's environment: the program gets the host's environment with each entry of `Env` replacing or adding its key. `Dir` is the working directory, `""` for the caller's. `Stdin` is the whole of the standard input; the program reads end of file after it.
2. `WithEnv`, `WithDir` and `WithStdin` return a copy. `WithEnv` copies `Env`, so the command it is given keeps its own entries (the laws `WithEnvOverrides` and `WithEnvKeepsTheOriginal`).
3. A status that is not 0 is a `Result`, not a failure: a CLI matches on it. A program that a signal ended has the status 128 plus the signal's number, as a shell reports it. `Stdout` and `Stderr` hold all the program wrote, read to their end.
4. The failures are a closed row of three, for a program that did not start. `NotFound` is a name not on the `PATH`, a path with no file at it, or the empty name (Go's `exec.ErrNotFound`, `fs.ErrNotExist`). `Denied` is a file the process may not run, a directory included, as Go's `LookPath` reports it (`fs.ErrPermission`). `Failed` is every other reason, with the host's text, such as a `Dir` that does not exist (`dir /x: no such file or directory`). An uncaught one is written with its generated text and status 1, such as `error: exec.NotFound{Name:git}`. The law `NotFoundNamesTheProgram` states that `NotFound` names the program.
5. **The stop protocol.** The Go body is `v1.ExecRun(t *rt.Task, name, args, env, dir, stdin)`. Its `runtimeBindings` row waits on the caller's task (`callerWait`); the stop of that task is its cancel hook ([Host packages](#host-packages)):
   - The program starts in a process group of its own (`Setpgid`), which holds it and each process it starts that does not leave the group.
   - `Run` returns only after the program is reaped and its output is read.
   - A stop of the calling task sends `SIGKILL` to the group, waits until the program is reaped, and ends the call with an interruption. So the caller's exit actions run after the program is gone. Inside a mask the wait is uninterruptible, and the program runs to its end.
   - After the program exits, `Run` sends `SIGKILL` to what is left of its group.
   - After either kill, `Run` waits until the group is empty: `SIGKILL` only marks a process, the kernel ends it later, and the host's reaper then reaps it. A process keeps the group's id until it is reaped, so `kill(-group, 0)` fails with `ESRCH` exactly when no process of the group is left, zombies included. The wait is at most 500 ms (for a slow reaper, or a process in an uninterruptible wait, which has `SIGKILL` pending and runs no code of its own again); an empty group costs no wait. So a call leaves no process of the group, not even a zombie (measured: without the wait, a killed grandchild was still in the run state 22 µs after `Run` returned). A process left in the group that holds stdout or stderr open delays the return by at most 500 ms (as Go's `WaitDelay` does), and `Run` then returns the status and the output read so far. A program that must outlive the call leaves the group itself (`setsid`).
   - The body signals only the group of the program it started, whose id is the program's pid, and only while it holds it. `Run` waits for the program's end without reaping it (`waitid` with `WEXITED|WNOWAIT` on Linux; a kqueue `EVFILT_PROC`/`NOTE_EXIT` on Darwin and the BSDs, where `waitid` also returns on a stop, golang/go#19314). An unreaped program, alive or a zombie, keeps its pid and so the group's id, so no other group can have that id at either kill. `Run` reaps the program after the kill, and then waits for the group's end with `kill(-group, 0)`, which sends no signal. `Run` reads the output from pipes it owns, so it can wait for the output's end before the reap. `TestExecStopModel` checks, at each kill, that the program is still unreaped in its group (a test hook).
   `TestExecStopModel` runs 120 generated programs (output, grandchildren that close or hold the output, exits by status and by signal, a block) with the caller stopped before the call, while the program runs, inside a mask, or never, against a model of the states `Running`, `Exited(status)` and `Killed`: the result, the output, the reap of the program and the end of every process in its group. Lower `TestExecRuns` stops a task once its program has started, and the program never reaches the line after its 20 s sleep; the check is that order, not a wall-clock bound.
6. A program implements `exec.Runner`: no std extern returns it, so it is open (`check.SealedStd`). `exec.Script` is the std's test provider: `Run` gives the `Result` that `Results` holds at `Key(c)`, or `NotFound` (the law `ScriptReplaysRecorded`). `Key(c)` is the name and each argument with a NUL byte before each argument, made by one `strings.Join` (`exec` imports `strings` and `slices`); no program's name or argument holds a NUL byte, so two commands have one key exactly when their names and arguments are equal. The law records the arguments `arg, arg` and the one argument `arg + "," + arg` side by side, so a key whose separator a program can write makes it false. The environment, the directory and the standard input are not in the key. A test binds `with context { exec.Runner: exec.Script{Results: …} } { run() }` in the place where `main` binds `exec.NewHost()`.
7. The six records are the `runtime/v1` structs `v1.ExecNotFound`, `v1.ExecDenied`, `v1.ExecFailed`, `v1.ExecResult`, `v1.ExecLine` and `v1.ExecChunk`, by the binding table (Types 4), so `exec.NotFound` and `fs.NotFound` are two Go types.
8. Speed: process start dominates a run. `BenchmarkExecRunTrue` holds `Run` of `true` to Go's `exec.CommandContext(ctx, "true").Run()`, and `BenchmarkExecRunMiB` holds a run that writes 1 MiB to a hand `bytes.Buffer` read and its `String()`. The body copies each stream once into its string, as the Go control does; no `unsafe` (std-align section 4: no measured gain over process start).
9. **Start** (CU4). `Runner.Start(c, s)` starts `c` and returns once it has started; the caller owns the `Running` (a resource: [resources](resources.md) R1, R10). Each stream goes to its sink: `Inherit` is this process's stream, `Discard` the null device, `ToFile{Path, Append}` a file the program writes itself (created when missing, emptied first unless `Append`; the bytes reach the file as the program writes them), and `ToCaller` the caller's `Lines`. A file sink that cannot be opened fails `Failed` with the reason `stdout PATH: REASON`, and nothing starts; a stopped caller starts nothing.
10. **Lines and Wait.** `Lines()` gives each line of the `ToCaller` streams as it comes, without its line feed (`Split` states the rule: a last line with no line feed is a line), stdout's and stderr's in the order they come, each stream in its own order, until both end. At most 64 lines wait for the caller: then the program waits in its write, so the caller's reading paces it. A `break` leaves the rest for the next `Lines`. One merged `Seq` and not two: a caller that read stdout to its end while stderr filled would wait forever. `Wait()` waits for the program's end and gives its status (128 plus the signal for a program a signal ended); it drops the lines not yet read, so it cannot wait on a full buffer, and `Lines` after it gives none. A stop of the caller ends either wait with the interruption and leaves the program to its owner.

**Chunks (CU4c).** `Chunks()` gives the bytes of the `ToCaller` streams as each read returns them, with no wait for a line feed: a `Chunk`'s `Text` holds any bytes, line feeds included, and a last fragment with no line feed comes as it was written, so `x` and `x\n` differ. After each stream's last bytes comes one `Chunk` with empty `Text`, its end. Where a stream is cut is not a promise; each stream's bytes and their order are. At most 64 chunks (each at most 32 KiB) wait for the caller, so the caller's reading paces the program. `Lines` reads the same chunks (one reader, one path): it keeps the bytes after a stream's last line feed until the next chunk of that stream or its end, and on a `break` or a stop it puts back what it read and did not give, so the next `Lines` or `Chunks` starts there and a caller may mix the two. A tee is the caller's own loop, not a sink:

```go
func tee(r exec.Running) string needs io.Stdout {
	let mut text = ""
	for c in r.Chunks() {
		if !c.Stderr {
			io.Stdout.Print(c.Text)
			text = text + c.Text
		}
	}
	text
}
```

`TestRunningMatchesModel` holds it against a fake process whose reader must pass each read on before it reads again: fragments with and without line feeds, `Lines` with a break and with a stop mid-read, `Chunks` that finds every byte written so far, and a read to the end that gives every byte and one end per stream. `TestExecStartRuns` runs a tee on the host. The Go control is a `Read` loop that sends each read's bytes on a channel; `Lines` is that loop's consumer with `strings.IndexByte`, as `bufio` would split it. No speed claim. Effect's `ChildProcess` gives stdout as a byte `Stream` and decodes lines on top; Elixir's `Port` delivers `{:data, binary}` messages as read and line mode only on request; Gleam's `shellout` buffers to the end; ReScript binds Node's `data` events of `Buffer`s. Ergo takes the byte stream as read and the line view over it, and leaves out a decoder pipeline: `Text` is a byte string, and `Lines` is the one decoder std needs.
11. **The release** (`Close`, run by the owner's scope end on every exit, a stop and a timeout included, masked, R9): a program that still runs gets SIGTERM to its group when `c.Grace` is above 0, then SIGKILL when it has not ended within `Grace`; `Grace` 0 sends SIGKILL at once. Then, as `Run` does after a program's end: the output is read to its end for at most 500 ms, SIGKILL goes to what is left of the group, the program is reaped, the release waits until no process of its group is left, and the sinks' files are closed. Each signal goes to the group while its leader is unreaped. `Run`'s stop follows the same grace rule. A timeout is the same path: `timeout.After(1h) { let r = try Runner.Start(c.WithGrace(15s), s); r.Wait() }` ends the program by `r`'s release when the deadline stops the block. A follower is a `Running` the caller leaves: `for l in r.Lines() { … if done { break } }`, and the scope's end ends `tail -f`. A tee reads `ToCaller` lines and writes each where it must go.
12. `Script.Start` replays the `Result` recorded for `c`: `Stdout` as one chunk and its end, then `Stderr`'s, each when its sink is `ToCaller` (so `Lines` gives `Split(Stdout)`'s lines, then `Split(Stderr)`'s), and `Status` from `Wait`; it writes no file and nothing to this process's streams. `Running` is sealed (the `Start` externs return it), so a program's own `Runner` gives a `Script`'s. The law `StartAgreesWithRun` states that a started program read to its end gives `Run`'s lines and status; `TestExecLaws` runs it against a `Script`. `runtime/v1` `TestRunningMatchesModel` holds the lifecycle against a model over a fake process (the signals, the status, one reap and no signal after it, each stream's order, no line after `Wait`, a stopped caller's `Lines` and `Wait`); `TestExecStreams` is the acceptance check on the host (a helper writes on command; after each command the file or the caller's lines hold exactly what was written; the release ends the group, by SIGTERM or after the grace); lower `TestExecStartRuns` runs the surface in a built program. The Go type is `*v1.Running`; `Line` is `v1.ExecLine`. *Prior art:* Effect's `ChildProcess` gives stdout as a `Stream` and `"inherit"`, and its scope kills the process; Elixir's `Port` sends lines as messages and `System.cmd(into:)` collects into a `Collectable`; Gleam's `shellout` has no streamed form; Go's `os/exec` gives `StdoutPipe` and an `*os.File` as `Stdout`, which `ToFile` uses so the bytes never pass through Ergo. Ergo takes the scope's kill and the inherited stream, not Effect's `Stream` type (a `Seq` a `for` reads is the language's own iterator) and not a collect-into protocol.

*Prior art:* Go's `exec.CommandContext` kills only the process it started, by `cmd.Cancel`, and `WaitDelay` bounds the wait for output a grandchild holds; Ergo kills the whole group, as a shell's job control does. Effect's `ChildProcess` spawns in a scope that kills the process when the scope closes, and gives `exitCode` as a value, not a failure. Elixir's `System.cmd/3` returns `{output, status}` with a status that is not 0 as data, and takes `env:` and `cd:` options; Gleam's `shellout.command(run:, with:, in:, opt:)` returns `Result(String, #(Int, String))`, which makes a status that is not 0 an error; ReScript binds Node's `child_process.spawnSync`, whose result holds `status`, `signal`, `stdout` and `stderr`. Ergo returns the status as data, as Elixir and Effect do, and keeps the failures for a program that did not start.

**Stop and status (CU4b).** `Running.Stop() int takes` runs the same masked grace, group shutdown and reap as `Close`, then returns the child's actual status using `Wait`'s status rule. It consumes the handle; a later use is S108. `Close` stays the scope-release form. A `timeout.After` body is escaping and runs on a child task (ESC1), so it cannot borrow a `Running` the caller owns: `timeout.After(1h) { r.Wait() }` is S108 (resources R31, R33). The wait with a deadline is `exec.WaitFor` (below).

**WaitFor (S-tz).** `func WaitFor(p Running, d duration) int fails timeout.Expired needs Scheduler`, called `p.WaitFor(d)`, waits at most `d` for the program's end and gives its status by `Wait`'s rule, or fails `timeout.Expired` when the deadline passes first. The deadline is the `Scheduler`'s time, as `timeout.After`'s is, so a test's `time.Manual` or `testing.VirtualClock` decides it. `Expired` leaves the program running, unsignalled, with its unread bytes for `Lines` and `Chunks`: the caller still owns `p`, and the idiom of a wait and then a stop reads

```go
let code = p.WaitFor(5s) catch {
case timeout.Expired:
	p.Stop()
}
```

A program that has ended gives its status at once and arms no deadline, whatever `d` is (law `WaitForEndedGivesStatus`). A program that ends as the deadline passes may give `Expired`; `Stop` then gives its status. A stop of the caller ends the wait, as `Wait`'s. `p` is a lend, not a move (`std.ArgClauses`): the body, `v1.ExecWaitFor`, waits on the caller's task when the program has ended and otherwise runs `v1.After` over `p`'s `Wait` on a child task, which `After` stops and joins on every exit before it returns, so the lend ends with the call. This is sound for `Running` and not for every resource: `Wait` waits on channels and its finish runs once, so it is safe on any task, where `timeout.After`'s escaping body must also refuse task-affine resources such as `tasks.Set` (ESC1). The ownership rules stay as they are: no structured lend into `timeout.After`'s child, which would need a per-type fact about task affinity. `TestRunningMatchesModel` (runtime/v1) runs `WaitFor` with a deadline that passes at once, before and after the program's end, against its model: before, `Expired`, no signal, every unread byte kept; after, the status and no deadline armed. Lower `TestExecStartRuns` (`postStopStatus`) waits with `WaitFor(0ns)` on a program that waits on a fifo, catches `Expired`, and stops it to read its TERM trap's 37. The Go control is a `select` on the process's exit and a `time.After`; `WaitFor` adds one child task, as `timeout.After` does. No speed claim. *Prior art:* Effect's `Effect.timeout` around a process's `exitCode` interrupts the wait and leaves the process to its scope, as here; Go's `exec.Cmd` has no wait with a deadline (`CommandContext` kills the process on the context's end; `WaitDelay` bounds only the output); Elixir's `Port` and `receive … after` give the message or the timeout and leave the port open; Gleam's `shellout` has no deadline; ReScript binds Node's `spawnSync`, whose `timeout` kills the child at the deadline. Ergo keeps the program alive on `Expired`, so the caller chooses `Stop`, another `WaitFor`, or the scope's release.

`TestRunningMatchesModel` also drives Stop before output, mid-stream and after exit, with arbitrary TERM-trap statuses and a stopped caller. `TestExecStartRuns` checks Stop's status with a real TERM trap that exits 37. The direct Go control is `Close`'s shutdown followed by the stored exit status; Stop adds no process or extra wait. No speed claim is made. Effect's scoped child process solves the same cleanup need; Ergo's consuming operation combines termination and status observation so a caller need not keep an unowned exit-code handle or signal a raw pid.

## Testing

[#209](https://github.com/cevr/ergo/issues/209). `testing.VirtualClock` provides `time.Clock` and `Scheduler` and jumps to the next timer when every task in the test is blocked (batch 12, C1; built). `testing.Script[S]` is a provider of any capability, derived from its operations, with answers the test sets and a record of calls (L5b; built). `T.Idle()` reports the tasks that keep a test from reaching idle (not built). The test harness is an ordinary `context`, so production code needs no change to run on virtual time.

| Declaration | Meaning | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `type VirtualClock { line time.Timeline }`; `func NewVirtualClock(sec int64) VirtualClock` | A test `time.Clock` and `Scheduler` whose time is `sec` Unix seconds at the start. | `NewVirtualTimeline(t *rt.Task, sec, nsec int64) *Timeline` (time.go), over `rt.NewAuto` (rt idle.go) |
| `Now(c VirtualClock) time.Time`, `Sleep(c VirtualClock, d duration)` | The clock's time; a sleep on it. | `Timeline.Read`, `Timeline.Wait` |
| `law SleepAdvancesExactly(n int64)`, `law IdleOnlyAdvance(n int64, k int)` | The laws ([Std laws](#std-laws)). | `TestTestingLaws` (runtime/v1) |
| `type Script[S] {}`, made as `testing.Script[S]{}` | A test provider of the capability `S`, derived from `S`'s operations: one field per operation, of type `Op[A, F]` (rules 7 to 12). | a generated Go struct per instance, one `*v1.Op[A, F]` field per operation, and an adapter per operation type it converts to (lower script.go) |
| `type Op[A, F] { Answer(f F), Calls() []A }`; `type NoArgs {}` | One operation of a Script: the answer the test set and the arguments of each call so far. | `*v1.Op[A, F]`: `NewOp`, `Answer`, `Calls`, `Call`; the defect `NoAnswer` (script.go) |
| `law ScriptRecordsEachCall(ks []string, n int)`, `law ScriptAnswersByTheLastAnswer(k string, a int, b int)` | The laws ([Std laws](#std-laws)). | `TestStdLawsRun` (lower) |

1. **Domain.** The clock's domain is the task that calls `NewVirtualClock`, from that call on, and every task started below a task of the domain. A task that made an earlier VirtualClock leaves that clock's domain. Tasks started before the call are outside it: the clock does not wait for them.
2. **When the time moves.** The clock counts the live tasks of its domain and those parked in a wait it sees: a `Sleep` on the clock, a queue's `Push`, `Pop` or `Values` step, a `Task`'s `Wait` and `Stop` (and a frame's drain of its tasks), and `timeout.After`. When the counts are equal and a sleeper is armed, the time moves to the earliest deadline, and every task asleep until that deadline wakes, in the order they slept. It never moves while a task of the domain can run.
3. **A host wait keeps the time still.** A wait on anything else, such as a read of the host's input or a file, a sleep on the host or on a `time.Manual`, or a lock, is not parked. So the clock does not move past it: a test that feeds standard input sees no timeout fire while the read waits.
4. **No lost window.** A waiter parks in the same step that checks its event, under the clock's lock, and the waker clears the parked flag of the task it wakes before the event is visible (the queue's signal, the end of a task, a timer, a stop). The Quint model of the batch-12 C1 unit checks the protocol (`time moves only when all are idle`, `no wake-up before its deadline`, `no lost wake-up`); `TestVirtualClockModel` (runtime/v1) runs generated programs of 1 to 5 tasks against a discrete-event model, 300 seeds.
5. **Time.** `Now` is `time.Unix` of the clock's reading: the Time has no monotonic reading, and the clock's wall time never falls. The clock is the one interface every machine timer reads ([runtime.md, Machines and actors](runtime.md#machines-and-actors) 9.6, one clock): `Scheduler.Sleep` and `time.Clock.Now`. `machines.RunTimed` reads both, and a live runner sleeps on them, so a machine's timers and a test's sleeps run on one virtual time with no change.
6. **Laws.** `SleepAdvancesExactly`: a lone `Sleep(d)` moves the clock by exactly `d`. `IdleOnlyAdvance`: a task that pushes while its parent sleeps reads the start time, and the parent wakes at its deadline.
7. **Script.** `testing.Script[S]` is a provider of the capability `S` that a test writes no code for (#237 stage 5, L5b). `S` is a named operation type with operations and written type arguments (`Script[Store[Job]]`). A type parameter, a type that is not a capability, a sealed std type, and a capability with an operation that returns a `Task` or takes a function are S108 at the type argument: "testing.Script[T]: T is a type parameter; …". The record of calls keeps each argument past the call, so a Script's operation never takes an argument that a call only lends (one rule, q39; #197 L8 C4): a function (a non-escaping function value, types.md, Function values 2), a borrow `*T` ("its operation Fill takes the borrow b *Box, …"; types.md, Borrows 1), and a value that holds a resource, taken or lent ("its operation Use takes the resource c Conn, …"; [resources.md](resources.md#lending)), since the record would be a second owner. The rule is decided at the type argument, a `Close` declared later included. A Script field of a generic record names a concrete capability (`Script[Use[T]]` is S108: "Use[T] is not a capability"), so an instance of the record, `Rig[Conn]`, adds no Script to decide (checked: `TestScriptDiagnostics`; the generic refusal, `TestGenericCapabilityDiagnostics`).
8. **Derived record.** The checker derives the record of `Script[S]` from `S`'s declared operations, as it derives a codec (no name list, no reflection): one field per operation, in `S`'s order and with its name, of type `Op[A, F]`. `A` is the operation's arguments: the one parameter's type, a tuple of several (`Put(k string, v int)` records `(string, int)`), `NoArgs` for none. `F` is the operation's signature as a function type, its rows included (`Get(k string) int fails Absent` gives `func(k string) int fails Absent`). The fields are `S`'s package's, so an operation hidden from a package is a field hidden from it.
9. **Making one.** `testing.Script[S]{}` makes one with no answers and no calls; a literal that sets a field is S108 ("a testing.Script[S] is made with no fields, …"). Every copy of a Script shares its operations (each `Op` is a synchronised handle, runtime.md, Tasks rule 6), so the test keeps its Script and binds a copy in a context: `with context { Photos: s } { run() }`.
10. **Providing S.** A `Script[S]` implements every operation type whose operations `S` has with `S`'s signatures (types.md, Interface assignability 1), so it converts to `S` wherever a value of `S` is expected, a context entry included. A call of an operation records its arguments (`s.Get.Calls()` is a copy, oldest first), then calls the answer that `s.Get.Answer(f)` set last, with the caller's task; the answer's failure is the operation's failure. A call of an operation with no answer is the defect `testing.Script[main.Photos]: Get has no answer`, except an operation with no result and no failure, whose call is only recorded.
11. **Lowering.** `Op[A, F]` is the runtime's `*v1.Op[A, F]`: a mutex, the calls and the answer. `Script[S]` is a generated Go struct with one field per operation; its conversion to `S` is an adapter whose method for each operation takes the lock once to append the arguments and read the answer, then calls the answer outside the lock (an answer may call the Script again). Hand Go control: a fake struct with a mutex, a slice of calls and a func field per method, as Go's `mockgen` writes; the same work per call (inferred, not measured).
12. **Laws.** `ScriptRecordsEachCall`: a Script records the arguments of every call of each operation in order, and an operation with no result and no failure needs no answer. `ScriptAnswersByTheLastAnswer`: each call gets the answer set last before it, its failure included, and a call with no arguments records `NoArgs`. Not built: a call that waits, pending, until the test answers it (the scene driver of #225 TEA-2).
13. **Machine laws are searched, not sampled** (X1; [types.md, Laws](types.md#laws) 13). A reachability law of a machine whose starts and signals can be listed and whose state has a key is decided by a breadth-first search of the machine's graph (runtime/explore), within `ergo test --states N` states (1,000,000 by default; a count of states, never a time); `ergo test --law NAME` runs that law alone, and `--skip-law NAME` all but it (X1b). The report says "proved" only when the whole graph was seen; a search the budget stops passes with "not proved" and the run length it checked. A refuted `never reaches` prints its shortest run as an Ergo `test` that fails by itself, and a replay command. So a protocol design written as an Ergo machine, with each invariant a variant its step enters and each mutant a second machine whose law must be refuted, is checked by `ergo test` in a small scope. An invariant can also be a pure predicate on the state (X2): `law Safe always safe` is decided as `never reaches` a state where `safe` is false, and `law CanDrop sometimes dropped` as `reaches` one where `dropped` holds; a sometimes law prints its witness as an Ergo `test` that passes by itself, and with no witness in a search the budget stopped it fails as "not found" ([types.md, Laws](types.md#laws) 13).

### Explored schedules

`ergo test FILE --explore MODE` runs the tests under many schedules of the controlled scheduler ([runtime.md, Explored schedules](runtime.md#explored-schedules)). The test binary is built once with the `ergoexplore` tag; each schedule runs in a worker process of it, and the search on the `ergo` side picks the task at each scheduling point (`runtime/explore/sched`).

| Mode | Flags | What it does | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Bounded search | `--explore dfs [--preempt B] [--schedules S]` | Depth-first over schedules with partial-order reduction on the dependency table below, at most `B` preemptions (default 2) and `S` schedules (default 10,000). The search under a bound keeps no sleep sets (item 2) | `ok explore dfs: proved for at most B preemptions (…)` when the search ended; `no failure in S schedules; not proved` when the budget stopped it |
| PCT | `--explore pct [--depth D] [--schedules S] [--seed N]` | `n` tasks and `k` steps come from a first, cooperative run; each schedule gives the tasks random priorities and `D - 1` change points (default depth 3, 100 schedules) | `no failure in S schedules (pct, depth D)` |
| Random walk | `--explore random [--schedules S] [--seed N]` | A uniform pick at each point | `no failure in S schedules (random)` |
| Replay | `--schedule TOKEN` | Runs the one schedule a failure printed | the run's own report |

1. **Dependency table.** Two steps of one task are dependent. A push, pop, close or write is dependent with every step on its handle; a read with every change of its handle; reads commute. A task's start is local: dependent only with a barrier. A spawn, join, stop, sleep, await, end and a resume after a wake are barriers, dependent with every step, since their effect is not one handle's. `TestDependencyTableTotal` keeps the table equal to `rt.OpKind`.
2. **The search is sound for its bound.** `TestDPORFindsEveryOutcome` (runtime/explore/sched) checks it against a naive enumeration of every schedule, on generated programs of 2 to 4 tasks on queues, inboxes and a `Ref`: the same set of outcomes, deadlocks included, with no bound and with bounds 0 to 2, and no more schedules. A race adds a choice at its earlier step (Flanagan and Godefroid's rule with vector clocks); under a bound the choice goes to the start of that task's stretch too, and no sleep set is kept (Coons et al.'s bounded POR).
3. **Workers.** A worker runs schedules back to back while each ends with every task ended. A run that stops, deadlocks, leaves its schedule or escapes the scheduler ends its worker, whose process group is reaped; the next run starts a new one. So a worker never holds the stacks of an abandoned run, and a search starts at most one worker more than the runs it abandoned (`TestPoolReplacesAbandonedWorkers`, `TestWorkerRetainsNoTask`). Each worker runs under the test memory limit.
4. **A failure.** The report prints the failing run's steps in words, the failing test again as a test that starts with `explore.Replay(schedule)` and the window of the schedule that the test ran, and `replay: ergo test FILE [--run NAME] --schedule 'TOKEN'` (the token is quoted: its stars are glob characters to a shell). A step names a task by its spawn path, and a handle by its name in the program: a task as `task 0.1`, a queue, a `Ref` or an actor (and its inbox) as the Ergo call that made it at its source position, relative to the folder of the program's files: `step 9: task 0.1: read on sync.NewRef at memory/fetch.ergo:39:14`. A second handle made by one call in the run, as in a loop, is `… (2)`. A handle the runtime made for itself (a scope, the source of a wake) keeps the number the run gave it, `#5`. A task's own start, spawn and end name no handle. The printed test needs `import explore`; under `ergo test FILE --explore` it fails by itself. A failure in a law or a property prints no test, only the command.
5. **One test or law.** `--run NAME` selects one test or law of the package by name, as it does for a plain `ergo test` (one flag, read by the test binary): the search explores that test's schedules only, the failing test is that test, and the replay command keeps `--run NAME`. A name the package does not have is a usage error (exit 2).
6. **Counters, no clock.** Each verdict prints the schedules, the scheduling points over all of them, the longest schedule, the runs a sleep set blocked and the workers started. No mode reads a clock; no test counts how often a random mode finds a failure.

| Declaration | Meaning | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `func Replay(schedule []string)` (package `explore`) | The next points of the explored run follow `schedule`, paths relative to the caller (`"0"` the caller, `"0.k"` the k-th task it starts after the call). A point whose task is not enabled stops the run with a defect. Under a plain `ergo test` it does nothing. | `ExploreReplay` (runtime/v1 explore_on.go; explore_off.go is empty) |

*Prior art (explored schedules):* Loom explores schedules depth first with DPOR and a preemption bound (`LOOM_MAX_PREEMPTIONS`); Concuerror uses source sets and sleep sets on the BEAM and replays from its linear trace; Coyote and P sample with PCT and replay a `.schedule` file; shuttle prints a schedule string for `replay`. Each needs the program to use its types or a rewritten binary; Ergo's seam is its runtime, so the test is unchanged. Gleam and Elixir reach Concuerror through the BEAM; ReScript has no scheduler to control.

*Prior art:* Go 1.25 `testing/synctest` moves a bubble's clock when every goroutine in the bubble is durably blocked, which the Go runtime knows; Ergo's runtime learns it from its own waits. Tokio `time::pause()` auto-advances when the runtime is idle; Trio `MockClock(autojump_threshold=0)`. Effect's `TestClock` moves only on `TestClock.adjust` (Ergo's `time.Manual`). Gleam, ReScript and Elixir lower no virtual clock: on the BEAM and in JavaScript it is a library over the host scheduler. *Effect workarounds replaced:* `TestServices` and `TestContext` layers become an ordinary `context`; `Effect.fork` before `adjust` becomes no step at all, since the clock moves on idle.

*Prior art (Script):* the purpose, in each, is a fake that follows the contract, answers what the test says and records what the code did. Elixir's Mox defines a mock module per behaviour (`defmock`) and checks it against the behaviour's callbacks; `expect(Mock, :fun, fn … end)` sets an answer and `verify!` checks that each one ran. Go's `mockgen` writes a struct per interface ahead of the build, and testify's `mock.Called` records calls through reflection on a string name. Gleam and ReScript have no derivation: a test writes a record of functions by hand (Ergo's `tape` and `blind` before L5b). Effect has no derived test service: a test writes a `Layer.succeed(Tag, { … })` by hand. *Not taken:* Mox's global expectations and `verify!` (a Script is a value a test holds and reads, so no registry and no process ownership; a test asserts on `Calls()`); `mockgen`'s generate step (the checker derives the record, so it never drifts from `S`); testify's string names (`s.Get` is a field the checker resolves); Effect's `Layer.succeed` of a hand object (the context entry `S: s` binds it).
